CYCLING AND MOTORING.

Some Useful Hints For Present Abnormal Times.

A CHESHIRE HOLIDAY.

An extremely pretty run along typical Cheshire roads and through typical Cheshire scenery is described below. The scenery is very pretty, while the roads are excellent, and many of the places passed en route have interesting historical asso- ciations. The route I followed when I traversed this district a short while ago was : Warrington to Northwich. 11 miles; Cranage, 71; Nantwich, 19; Audlem. 8; Whitchurch, 81; Ches- ter, 20; Crabtree Green, 121; Warrington, 121; total. 981 miles. Leave Warrington by Market Gate and Bridge- street. over the Manchester Ship Canal for a good road to Stretton, being part of the famous thoroughfare between Land's End and John-o'- Groats. There are some very pretty views around Stretton, while the old half-timbered Hall is worth a visit. Care must be taken in approaching North- wich and in passing through the town, for the streets are very narrow, with sharp and dangerous turns. Northwich is famous for its salt mines, especially Marston Mine, which is 300 feet deep, the roof being supported by pillars of salt. Leave the town by Witton-street, then through typically English rural scenes to Cranage, with its fine hall and park, and on to Nantwich. an interesting and level ride, passing en route Condate (probably a Roman station) and Middlewich (an old historic town just midway between Northwich and Nant- wich) and leaving Crewe on the left. From Nant- wich to Audlem is a pretty ride, along a very good and level road; at A.udlem a right-angle turn is taken for Whitchurch, from whence an excellent twenty-mile run into Chester ensues. Chester has rightly been termed the Quaintest city in England, with its covered promenade above the ground floors in the Rows: At, the Market Cross four great main roads meet. The city walls, built by the Romans, are practically complete, while there are many 17th century timber houses. Eaton Hall (in the park of which cycling is permitted) is only 3/ and Hawarden Castle 6 miles away. Both are well worth visiting. Leave Chester by Foregate- street and make for Stamford Bridge, over the River Gowy; then on to Tarvin, the scene of many a struggle in the Civil War; Delamere. where there is still a little bit of the " Forest " left; and Crabtree Green, a pretty run through typical Cheshire scenery, and back to Warrington by Acton Bridge. Lower Whitley, and Stretton. RE-ENAMELLING WHEELS AND SPOKES. Nothing spoils the look of the machine more than chipped and rusty spokes and wheels, and yet because the wheels are subjected to the most mud and dust they are the first to go. Fortunately the re-enamelling of wheels and spokes is not nearly so difficult as the re-enamelling of the frame. It is possible to get a finish almost as good as if it had been stove-enamelled with just ordinary materials and without the aid of heat. The

specially prepared cycle enamels are good for the purpose, but a more lasting and brilliant finish can be secured by applying two coats of ordinary fiat carriage paint and finishing off with a coat of carriage varnish. Spirit enamels are not good, as they dry too quickly to allow one to finish the work properly. Before applying any paint thor- oughly clean off all old enamel and rust with emery cloth. Do the spokes first (one coat will. Perhaps, suffice for them). beginning at the cross- ing inside, going all round a few inches up, then completing the painting from the rim downwards; turning the wheel round, doing the insides front the reverse aide, and finally painting the outsides of the spokes in the same way. When painting the rim, tackle between the spokes first; then do the outside, running the brush smoothly and evenly round. THE USE OF PARAFFIN.

I have more than once seen cyclists squirting paraffin for all they were worth into the bearings. And then they grumble when their machine runs badly! Of course it does; it would be very sur- prising if it didn't. Paraffin has no lubricating value. It is a cleanser, as will be readily under- stood when I point out that it is merely a crude form of petrol. As a matter of fact, it is now mixed with petrol and used as fuel in motors. Avoid paraffin as a lubricant; it should not even be used to dilute oil that has thickened. If you want a new lubricant try sperm oil, which is used extensively in many engineering works: Its chief charms are that it is a metal preservative and a cleanser, as well as being a lubricant. Person- ally, however, I think it is difficult to beat any of the reliable lubricating oils at present on the market.

THEF PETROL SHORTAGE—A FEW TIPS. Motor cycling is a game of chance now. Woe betide the man who starts out with a half-empty petrol tank, expecting to get a fill-up somewhere on the road. The chances are that at every garage he will be told that only regular customers are supplied. The first tip, then, is always to start with a full tank, always to take a spare 'supply, and always to fill up whenever you do get the chance. If by chance you do run dry and no petrol is available, and you must have recourse to paraffin. do. your utmost to keep your engine hot until you can get started again. A hot engine. starts almost as easily on paraffin as on petrol, but a cold engine is usually the very Dickens to start on anything heavier than motor spirit. See that the paraffin is well strained as it passes into the tank: it is invariably dirty. If your engine has got cold before you can fill up with paraffin, if possible wrap a rag dipped in hot water round the carburettor and put in some good pedal work on the stand to start the engine running. Get it nicely hot before attempting to push off. Unless you are forced to, don't run to the last drain of petrol in the tank. Even a little petrol with the paraffin will ensure an easier start. CLEAN RADIATORS.

A guilty majority of car owners habitually drive with a faulty water circulation system. The engine over-heats when it is called upon for a lot of traffic work, or to climb a stiff hill, but never is the radiator suspected. It must be carbonisation, or poor petrol, or a faulty fan drive, says the motorist. If you want to see the choking effect water, particularly hard water, has on piping, examine the interior of an old water pipe. You will find it heavily coated with fur. The same effect is found in the tubes of the radiator, pump and other parts. The best way to ensure the circulation system always being clean is periodi- cally to flush it with a strong liquid that will remove all fur deposit. There are many Patent preparations on the market, but, to be candid, I have never found anything to beat strong soda- water—a pound of soda dissolved in two gallons of hot water. The soda-water is poured into the radiator, which is afterwards filled right up with plain water. The engine is then started up ap,i the water kept circulating for a quarter of an hour, the engine running slowly. Finally Om soda-water is thoroughly washed out and the radiator refilled with clean water. If, as a result of neglect, the furring has become very had indeed, it is possible that the soda-water will not remedy matters. Then recourse must be had to a solution of one-part hydrochloric 'acid to twenty-eight parts water, kept circulating for two or three minutes only, followed by the soda solution mentioned above, kept circulating for another few minutes. The washing after the soda solution has been drained off must be very thorough.

NO WORSE OFF.

Guardians And Allowances 01

Officials When Called Up.

Is the method the Board of Guardians have adopted regarding the payment of their soldier- officials an encouragement to obtain promotion from the ranks?

It is the custom to pay the difference between the money they receive in the Army and that which they used to get when in civil employ- ment.

By the Local Government Board (Emergency Powers) Act, 1916, it is now provided that "in a case where his (the official's) naval or mili- tary pay is increased in• consequence of his promotion they may, if they wish, disregard this increase to his pay and continue payment at the rate allowable before the promotion is received. The object of this provision is to enable the Guardians, if they think fit, to allow an employe to secure some pecuniary advantage as the result of his promotion." On this communication being read to the Board by the Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley), at their meeting on Wednesday, the fey. A. M. Mit- chell remarked: "By our system there is not much incentive for a man to gain promotion." The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley): What we want to see is that they are no worse off. Men don't go with the object of making money out of it. _ _ _

Mr. W. J. Shaw: The higher promotion a man gets the more money he requires to keep up his status. If his expenses increase ho has a right to he met in some way.

The Chairman: Looking at it as the chair- man of more than one Finance Committee, I hold that nobody has any just ground for com- plaint if by reason of his patriotic action his financial position is no worse off. Mr. Shaw: I quite understand, but if you change a man's circumstances his money should alter. There the discussion ended.

Mistress: "Indeed. How's that?" Charwoman: "Well, my man's earning so much now that there's plenty coming in. Last week we was obliged to put some in the savings bank. and I'm afraid we shall have to again this."—" Punch."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 3, 1916.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

"Tommy Warle.". Thus familiarly, and really affectionately, did old Yorkshire Methodists among whom I lived upwards of a generation ago style the Rev. Thomas Waugh, who preached the anniversary sermons at Sankey Bridges Chapel last Sunday. There may be more scholarly men in Wesleyan pulpits—Mr. " Warfe " makes no pretences in that direction—but during his lifelong service he has lost none of his earnestness and fervour as connexional evangelist; in that respect he will bear comparison with " Gipsy " Smith. His preaching is distinguished by the impassioned note of certainty common amongst the early Methodists, and to which Wesleyans have been accustomed in some later years, thanks to such , faithful and able exponents as Beaumont, Punshon, Gervase Smith, _Richard Roberts,

John S.. Workman, Jame,; Nance and others

Whom a section of my readers will easily call to mind.

As Revivalist.

In his capacity as connexional evangelist, Mr. Waugh continues to cling to the methods which he and his colleagues, Cooke and Davidson—the former particularly—found so successful about the period above indicated. Naturally enough, when conducting a week's mission in one of the chapels of the denomination, he especially wel- comes immediate and visible results. His striving for open manifestations occasionally leads to undue prolongation of meetings, with repeated appeals to young people to leave their pews and pass into the inquiry room. Gener- ally speaking, the time for that sort of thing seems to be going fast. A Severe Trial.

An expression of opinion on my part respect- ing the point just raised may by some be deemed a gratuitous impertinence, but I am sure the object of it would not himself resent criticism. As a matter of fact, I have all along been one of Mr. Waugh' s admirers, and in strong sympathy with the Church he serves so constantly, with heart and soul. I regard him as a splendid specimen of the robust and manly Christian. Some years ago the hearts of his friends through- out the country were suddenly thrilled by the news that, on the very threshold of a promising career, a highly ,accomplished son of his had been killed in a terrible railway accident. There was an ordeal for one of Mr. Waugh's profession, and he not only withstood the test as ordinarily understood, but triumphed to the extent of forthwith addressing to the signalman a letter which will always stand out in my memory as a model of rare charity. From Manse to Stage.

The appearance of a clergyman's daughter with a company at the Royal Court Theatre re- minded me of a great trial that befel another Methodist of the old school, whose training and convictions caused him to have the utmost horror of the drama and its associations. What must have been his feelings when one day he awoke to the fact that his only son had adopted the stage as his profession! One can imagine his sensations when in course of time the son came to be billed with his company to appear in the town in which his father was stationed. Members of the latter's Church scrutinised the sphinx-like countenance narrowly as he minis- tered to them, but the old man betrayed no out- ward sign of the emotions which sympathetic hearers were convinced were in conflict within. I suppose that in these days no such fears need be apprehended on the part of any concerned 'even in conditions otherwise identical. A Dilemma.

La Somna's most marvellous gift manifests itself in prompt replies to strings of questions calculated to reduce any ordinary mortal to a condition of helpless bewilderment. Even the common or garden street corner orator has his unpleasant moments, though to do him full jus- tice he seldom fails to "get a bit of his own back" when badgered by anyone in his crowd. Then there is that other type of interrogator, like the --- but a simple illustration may suffice. Here it is:— The lecturer lon theosophy had concluded his long and able address, and stood looking at the audience. "If there is any question," he Faid, "that any of you would like to ask me before I sit down, I should be pleased to answer it." Amid the deep silence that followed this remark an earnest looking man near the door rose up and said: "I'd like to know, professor, if anybody has ever yet discovered a reliable and certain cure for corns." Church Officers' Weaknesses.

The Archdeacon's charge at the Warrington Parish Church last week reminded me of a custom which at one country Church in a dis- trict in which I lived many years was persisted in until those who clung to it became somewhat of a byword and reproach. The churchwardens and sidesmen in question put a very precise and literal interpretation upon certain former duties of their ancient office which were men- tioned by the Archdeacon last week, and which 'they still preferred to observe in their own way. On Sunday mornings, as soon as the "To Deum " was reached, they passed out in solemn file to see to the "maintenance of order" in the quiet, deserted churchyard—in other words, to enjoy a surreptitious tmoke and a chat on things in general. The scandalised vicar did his utmost to break down this singular custom, but his officers were as adamant when thus assailed. In like manner Nonconformist deacons, trustees, &c., here and there in my itinerary have made themselves conspicuous by their habit of loitering and chatting outside long after service commenced.

Belfry Incident. The Archdeacon further pointed out that it was the duty of the churchwardens to see ,that the bells were'in good order. I was reminded of a rather alarming mishap in the belfry of a Parish Church where I was stationed several years ago. The then vicar had just looked in toward the close of one of the best " rings " ever accomplished there, when the rope of the big tenor bell broke. The ringers immediately stopped, but the big bell went on, the rope cracking up and down with loud reports. Even so, no corner of the belfry seemed safe, but when the rope caught and brought with it a long iron gas, pendant, the danger was greatly increased, the action simultaneously plunging the place in darkness. Some trembled for the fate of a valuable memorial window close by, but fortunately the sweep of the bell wheel soon subsided, and the incident closed without more serious mischief. No, Indeed!

- A ministerial friend of mine confides to me that it is very difficult always to say the right thing at a military service. He writes (for we are far apart now, and have had varied experiencim since we became acquainted in an industrial centre ir Yorkshire)--L-Preachers do not knwr exactly what to say to soldiers; that is, we ordinary preachers. Some try to compltment them; and tl,en are apt to flounder after the manner of the special preacher at a charity ,festival of friendless girls. With some • five hundred of these lassies seated in frcnt of him attired in their plain dull uniforms of grey homespun, the eloquent divine announced for his text, " say unto ye that Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these."

WORKHOUSE BEEF.

Contractors Want More Money, Rev. A. M. Nlitehell Wants Less Meat.

Rising prices of beef prompted Messrs. Single- ton and Co. to write to the Board of Guardians on Wednesday, and ask...: for an addition of ad. per lb. on their contract rate. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: If prices have gone up they ought to receive something. Mr. E. L. Bent: If we meet them in this way we shall have to meet others.

Mr. W. J. Shaw: We have done it before. We have done it for a farmer, so why shouldn't we do it for a butcher? Mr. W. C. Brooks: It is not fair to the others who tendered and did not get the contract. , The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Why not do without meat? I have done without it for years. Mr. Shaw: Are you ready with a proposition to that effect? But the Board took no notice of his badinage, and agreed that the matter should be consigned to the House Committee for them to deal with.

ODDFELLOWS' CONFERENCE.

Council Chamber Crowded With

Delegates.

THE MAYOR'S WELCOME.

The Council Chamber It. the Town Ball was just large enough to accommodate the delegates to the Independent Order of Oddfelloi,vs' (Man- chester Unity) Lancashire and adjacent dis- tricts conference on Saturday afternoon, there being about 170 delegates present. Before entering the hall, members of the Executive Council were received in his private parlour by the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock), who has been styled on many occasions "The Friendly Society Mayor," because of the deep interest he takes in these institutions. Before the conference proper began, the President, Bro. Charles Griffiths, P.P.G.M., of East Man- chester, introduced his Worship to tip whole of the delegates.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR AND THE BEAUTIES OF WARRINGTON.

In welcoming them to Warrington, the Mayor said that was the largest friendly society con- ference they had had in that room, and he was very pleased that the Manchester Unity had found it a sufficiently charming place. He was very proud of Warrington, and he had asked Mr. T. P.. Bradshaw, a leading Oddfellow in the town, which way he had brought the leading delegates from the Central Station to the Town Hall so that they could get the best impression of the town. He was told that Mr. Bradshaw had brought them along Horsernarket-street and Sankey-street. That was certainly better than having brought them along Queen-street, past the Fire Station and Legh-street, as most people did when they brought visitors to the Town Hall, but if he (the Mayor) had had anything to do with it, he would have taken them by a far pleasanter and impressive route—say. for instance, down Bridge-street, through the 'Queen's Gardens, Winmarleigh-street, and into the Park, for, by the time they had got to the Town Hall the delegates would have been re- marking upon what a beautiful place Warring- ton was. (Laughter, and applause.) Warring- ton was supposed to be a town of smoke. They were certainly very proud of their industries, but they were equally proud of their Town Hall and Park, which lay in the centre of them all. It was not every town which could, boast of such a happy circumstance. Friendly societies had always done good work not only in connection with their societies but in public capacities. It was only the other day that Mr. Bradshaw was appointed on the War Pensions Committee, and he (the Mayor) ventured to suggest that friendly society people were the kind that•were needed on such committees, because they had wise and sympathetic ideas of life. In conclusion, the Mayor recalled that he vris a benefit member of that society, as he also was of the Foresters. Whenever he was ill he could claim an allowance, belt sc.mehow or anotlat r they always forgot to put him on the funds. (Laughter.) Bro: Clare, P.G.M., of the Warrington dis- trict, extended a cordial welcome to the dele- gates, expressing the hope that they would leave Warrington with happy recollections of their visit.

For his kindness in allowing them the use of the Council Chamber, the Mayor was heartily thanked.

BUSINESS FOLLOWED BY CONCERT.

The delegates then discussed business in private, the main item being the question of soldiers' contributions while on active service. About 100,000 Oddfellows have joined the Colours already. After the meeting members of the executive were entertained to tea at Atkinson's Cafe by the officers and members of the Warrington district, the Mayor also being present. After- wards the executive and a large number of dele- gates adjourned to the "Constitution': Lodge, held at the Pelican Hotel, where a smoking con- cert took place, Bro. William E. Clare, Prov. G.M. presiding. Various toasts were honoured, that of "The Warrington District" being pro- posed by P.P.G.M. Charles Griffiths (president of the conference), Prov. C. C. Frank Webster responding. The speeches were interspersed with songs by P.P.G.M. Alban Boyle, P.G. Albert E. Hare, P.G. H. Kaye and P.G. Fred Hare; and recita- tions by P.P.G.M. George Hyde (Marple), and P.P.G.M. James Massey. Mr. John W. Pem- berton was at the piano.

BREAD GOES SOUR

When Mixed With Potatoes At The Workhouse.

WAR NOTION FAILURE.

A war notion has failed at the Workhouse, the experiment with potato bread having been dis- appointing in its results. The food goes sour. Made in small quantities for families potato bread is admittedly good, but the large amount necessary for the Workhouse is regarded by the Master (Mr. W. Loxham) as unpalatable. Other Unions which have tried the now method have come to a similar conclusion, as Mr. Loxham's report to the Board of Guardians on Wednesday, shows: "I have made a trial of the recipe for bread-making which Mr. Elias (Local Govern- ment Board Inspector) has sent, and I have made inquiries from other Unions. I prefer to keep to our own method, and, with the excep- tion of Wigan, other Unions are of the same opinion. Salford Guardians have given the potato bread a lengthy trial, and at first were very well satisfied, but they found out that it would not keep, and much was wasted because it was sour. They have now changed its use. Our trial was with 2801 b. flour, 401 b. potatoes, and 11b. berm. The bread yielded was 3841 b., whilst from the same amount of flour with our own method we can get 3961 b.; and the potato bread was by no means as good as the old method. The baking of our bread is most satis- factory, and I do not wish to change it, unless a material advantage may be effected thereby?' A Surprising Result. .

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell was rather surprised to hear the result of the experiment, and won- dered whether -the quality of the potatoes hail anything to do with the inferiority of the bread. Some of the farmers on the Board might tell them.

Mr. George Mather: I don't think it is worth worrying about, for the price of potatoes has gone up to a high pitch, while the price of flour is coming down. Mr. E. L. Bent: Next time perhaps Mr. Lox- ham will try different classes of flour. lam rather surprised to hear the result, because I always contend that bread is better with a few potatoes. (Hear, hear.)

PENSIONS COMMITTEE

FUSS.

Guardians Claim To Be Among Representative Bodies.

Hoping they are not too late in making their application, the Board of Guardians think they should have a share in the work of the War Pensions Committee.

- --- This ambition was mooted by Mr. W. C. Brooks at a meeting of the Board of Guardians on 'Wednesday. "For the last few weeks," he observed, "I notice that there has been a lot of fuss about representatives on the War Pensions Committee, but I notice there has been no fuss as to who should represent the Guardians. I consider that a committee of that character, .having the duties which will devolve upon it, should have representation of the Poor Law on it. We have either been overlooked, or some. 'thing else, in not being asked to send a repre- sentative. All sorts of people have been ap- pointed—Town Councillors, Friendly Societies, Soldiers' and Sailors' Families, Labour men Mr. W. J. Shaw: Charlie Dukes! (Laughter.)

Mr. Brooks (continuing): I do protest that we as have not been consulted, and I hope it will not be too late. I think it ought to go forward the unanimous wish of the Board that we should be represented. I move a resolution to that effect.

Miss Broadbent (promptly): I second it. The Rev. A,. M. Mitchell (with equal speed and enthusiasm): We'll till second it. That was against the Standing Orders, but all voted for the resolution.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
£1 FOR 15s. 6D.

The Duty Of Saving To Pay The

Cost Of The War.

Mr. A. J. Jolley presided over a meeting of the LoOal Central Committee for War Savings held at the Town Hall on Thursday night, when Mr. Wilfrid Rowland, the hon. secretary, ex- plained that two associations had been formed in Warrington, under the Government scheme for saving and investing in a manner that will assist the country to pay the cost of the war. One of the associations has been formed by the municipal officers and the other by the students attending the Secondary School. The Honorary Secretary further explained that the limitation og the scheme to those with incomes of less than JX3OO has been withdrawn. An Executive Committee was appointed, in conjunction with Mr. Rowland, to receive ap- plications from associations for affiliation with the Local Central Committee.

It was decided to take steps to introduce the scheme to the notice of the scholars in elemen- tary schools and to the works in town by the valuable assistance, if it could be obtained, of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee. The Need and the Way to Meet It.

Our expenditure on the war has reached colossal dimensions, and the Government are desirous of raising the money otherwise than by floating loans. The nation's outgoings for the year total £1,825,000,000, of which 0E1,625,000,000 represents the cost of the war: This money has to be found by us, because there is prac- tically no other country who can help us. Of the needed money we are raising by taxation this year £500,000,000, two-and-a-half-times as much as we ever raised in taxation before; but still there remains £1,325,000,000 to be raised from the people, and it is desirable that it should be raised by all classes of the com- munity.

We an all do our share towards finding the necessary sinews of war. Saving In Groups.

The general scheme of the National War Savings Committee seeks to set up local Central Committees, whose two principal functions are to create an atmosphere in their area in which the necessities of the situation will be realised, and to encourage the setting up of loud War ,Savings Associations among different groups of people. These associations can be set up by any group of people, however small, accus- tomed to come together in ordinary times— trade unions, friendly soc'eties, co-operative societies, works' people attending the same place of worship, 'people' who work together in the same shop, Boy' Scouts, and Girl Guides. The groups manage their own affairs in demo- cratic fashion, being supplied free with the forms for saving and transferring money, and the keeping of accounts. There are two methods of saving—by exchequer bonds and by war savings certificates. War savings certificates are the best offer ever made to the people of a national security. They bear compound interest and rise in value: At the end of the first year, 158. 9d.• second year, 16s. 9d.; third year, 17s. 9d.; fourth year, 18s. 9d.; fifth year, £l. The simplest form of association is this : Thirty-one people agree to pay 6d a week. The secretary takes the thirty-one sixpences to the Post Office and buys a certificate with the 15s. Bd. • The next week he buys another with the second thirty-one sixpences, and so on. The question of who of the Ihirty-one should have the first certificate could be settled by them- selves. The money can be drawn out at any time, though, of course, the idea is to lend it at least to cover the peripd•W the war, during which it will -grow in value under the magic influence of -compound interest with Govern- ment security. -

Mr. Rowland, Guild of Help Offices, Win- marleigh-street, will be pleased to give infor- mation as to the formation of associations to anyone interested in the matter.

CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

Over £12,000 Increased Sales In One Quarter.

REDUCED DIVIDEND.

The 222nd quarterly meeting of the Warring- ton Equitable Industrial Co-operative Society, Ltd., was held in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo- street, on Monday. Mr. Upson presided, and about 250 members were present. Messrs. Handles, Shaw, Smith and Brown were elected scrutineers and returning officer. The President, in moving the adoption of the committee's report, pointed out that the sales had. again shown. a large increase, the total being .1.115,378, an increase of .t 12,444 over the corresponding period last year. The member- ship now stood at 15,652, a net increase of 194 on the last three months. Members were losing money by not trading with the butchery depart- ment. Mr. Upson explained the reasons that had led the committee to suggest a reduction in the interim dividend from 3s. 2d. to 35., and said that the prices of several of the articles in general use had been so reduced that in some instances even the expenses were not being paid. Members were obtaining a far greater total re- duction in prices than they were losing through the reduced dividend.

The report was adopted unanimously, no ex- ception whatever being taken to the reduction of the dividend.

During the brief discussion that followed the report, one of the lady members suggested the advisability of holding a series of open-air meet- ings in the outlying • districts, with a view to explaining the benefits of the society to those who are not already members, and the matter was promised consideration. Voting for three members of the committee resulted in the return of Messrs. G. Faulkner and J. H. Jones, Mr. Sherburn being elected to fill the vacancy caused by the retirement by rule of Mr. Packer.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
GRANTS AND INVESTMENTS.

The meeting approved the grant of ..t5. that had been made to the British Ambulance Com- mittee, although some members took exception to the money having been paid without the quarterly meeting being first consulted. The President pointed out that every grant was carefully considered, and that only in cases of urgency was money paid away before the quar- terly meeting's approval had been asked. He considered it was not right to tie the hands of the committee in a case of urgency, and to wait perhaps for three months, when they had every reason to believe the members would be fully in sympathy with the grant proposed to be' made. The investment of £l,OOO in 5 per cent. Gov- ernment Exchequer Bonds was approved.

FARM AND GARDEN.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL THE DIFFERENCE.

According to Mr. Stephen Reynolds, quoted by Mr. Acland in the House of Commons last week, a favourite song of the fisherman contains the following verse:— " " The husbandman has rent to pay, Blow, winds, blow;

And seed to purchase every day, Row, boys, row. But he who farms the rolling deeps Though never sowing always reaps, The ocean fields are fair and free, There are no rent-days on the sea."

Mr. Acland referred to complaints regarding the depredation of foxes, and attributed these to the fact that many gamekeepers had gone to the Front. Undoubtedly, he added, there would be a great increase of food supplies if there were n) foxes, but that would mean to many farmers a greater loss than all the destruction which the foxes caused among poultry.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
RAIN AND HAY.

• Never have too much grass down at once, writes ," Staffordshire " in this week's "Farm and Home." Under no conditions of weather is such a course justifiable, because it is impossible to makw,the hest-class hay in this way, and in awkward times whole acres are utterly spoilt. Another question which often calls for prompt solution at such timesis the work at which 'the haymakers should be set when the hayfield is too 'wet for them to enter. Perhaps on grass hold- ings the men cannot be better employed at such times than in repairing culverts under gate- ways, opening faulty ditches, and cleaning stopped drains. The latter class of work can only be attempted in the spring, summer and autumn, when there is not much, fear of being "flooded out" by the escape of pent-up moisture; and another argument in favour of such work being "faced" just now is the fact that the spots at which the stoppages occur are easily distinguished by the utthealthy colon of the grass.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
DAYLIGHT SAVING.

Some famers have not taken very kindly to the " newe'day " set up by Parliament, but they will like it better as they know more about it. In answer to some correspondents the Editor of the "Farm and Home" says There is ;no reason for assuming that the Government will interfere with anyone for " ignoring " the new measure. Farmers, like most other people, can begin their work when they please. There is nothing in the measure to prevent a man who formerly opened his office at 9, opening it now at 10 by the "new time," *which wo.uld be exactly the same hour. He would cause inconvenience mainly to himself, for most people with whom he does business would conform to the new regulation, and as a consequence he would lose an hour for business each day. The farmer's position is different; most of his work must be regulated by.thesun, and altering the clock Won't alter this. But, none the less, he cannot afford to " ignore " tho legislation, for reasons that are obvious.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
EMPLOYMENT OF SOLDIERS ON AGRI- CULTURAL WORK.

Farmers who desire to obtain the assistance of soldiers for work on the land are reminded that they should apply to the. local Board of Trade Labour Exchange (the address of which can be obtained from any Post Office) for the necessary application form. A soldier cannot be released to work continuously on a farm, and not more than four consecutive weeks' furlough will be given to any one man. If a farmer lives in the neighbourhood of a military station there is no objection to his applying direct to the Commanding Officer for the soldiers whom he requires at short notice and for a period not exceeding six working days. The other conditions of employment remain the same as was the case last year. Command- ing officers will meet the demand for soldier labour as far 'as they are able, but men under training cannot be released, nor those required for necessary military duties, and soldier; serving abroad cannot be brouga home fpr this purpose.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

TEN-WEEK STOCK.

These old-fashioned and sweetly-scented flowers are very often seen in very poor condi- tion, the owner wondering why such should be the case. The fault in most cases is that the soil is too poor, while the plants are growing far too closely to allow of full development. No annual better repays for the provision of a rich root run than the Ten-week Stock. Give the plants good deep soil to which has been added plenty of manure, for preference cow manure, and plenty of room, and the reward will be vigorous plants that will in their season give of their best in the way of handsome spikes of sweet-scented flowers.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
SUMMER LETTUCES.

It is a very difficult matter to keep up a con- stant supply of lettuces during the summer. To make sure of this a quite different mode of culture must be followed. If grown in the ordinary way, by transplanting, it will be found that the plants, as a rule, run to seed instead of hearting in. To make sure of a regu- lar supply the best way is to sow in small quan- tities, and at frequent intervals, in shallow drills in rows, as then the young seedlings can be more easily thinned. When, thinned the plants should stand at least from a foot to fifteen inches apart. If grown thus the plants have plenty of room to develop, and thus form solid hearts. Another-important point is to see that the ground is well manured, so that the plants may -grow away freely. For a light soil there is nothing so good .as cow manure, the value of this lying in the fact that it retains the moisture. If possible, it is always advisable rto select a partly shaded border. For the early summer crop a north-east or north-west border is a very suitable place, a north border being reserved for the hottest summer days. The advantage of sowing in drills is that it is in a. dry time far easier to water should this' be necessary, while liquid manure can also be given. The principal points in the growing of summer lettuces are thin sowing, early thinning, and, should the weather be dry, frequent watering through a mulch of manure; —Wick, in "Farm and Home."

Keeping Light Breeds at Home. Light breeds are extremely sqtive, and fre• quently cause a nuisance through flying into the neighbour's garden. 'The cure is simple. Cut five of the flight feathers in one wing. This does not in any way affect the beauty of the bird, since it can- not be seen and does not affect winter laying. as is thought by some poultry keepers. To Cure Hens of Broodiness.

The hatching season is practical ended, and broody hens should be cured ,as soon as possible. A coop with a 'slatted bottom or the ordinary crate will serve well for this purpose. Hens which are found on the nest after 6 p.m. may be regarded as broody, and should be .placed in the coop imm3di- ately they are found. Place the coop in the middle' of the run, and a cure should be affected in three Gr four days. Feed the birds vrell, and they will to laying again in two or three weeks. The Maio Birds.

As the breeding season is. over, male birds which are not intended for /text season's use should be disposed of at once. Where they are to be kept on for another year they should be separated from the hens, and given all the liberty possible.--" Peaitrx World."

===== 0002601_19160603_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESERVING EGGS.

General *most i ono.

1. Eggs for preservation should be treated as soon as possible after they are laid, but not until they have been cooled. If 'the egg is not abso- lutely fresh when placed in the preserving medium the final result cannot be entirely satisfactory. Als egg twenty-four hours old is _superior to one a. week 'old.

2. Eggs should not be treated in a warm place, and where Liinewater or waterglass is used the preparation should be quite cold before the eggs are placed in the solution.

3. Eggs from hens fed chiefly , upon grain, and with full liberty, are likely to keep better than those laid by fowls in confined runs. 4. The general experience has been that infertile eggs keep in geed cendition longer than those which contain a living germ. Probably this is less apparent when eggs are preserved at a lo* temperature. •

5. When eggs are preserved in water,glass or limewater the vessels containing them should be stored in a Cool place, at a temperature of not less than 33 degrees Fahrenheit, nor more than 45- degrees. A cool. well-ventilated cellar is excellent for this purpose. Exposure to a higher tempera- ture, even for a few hours, will cause deterioration in spite of the preservative.

5. Eggs may be stored in large or small quan- tities. and may be allowed to remain in the pickle for six months. Wooden, cement, or galvanised iron vessels should be employed. 7. Eggs should be carefully tested before they are preserved, and again prior to sale. For this purpose a well-constructed candling lamp is to be preferred; but a piece of black cardboard, 8 inches square, with an oval hole in the centre rather smaller than an ordinary egg, can be used. Each egg should be placed against the hole, and held between a strong light and the eye, so that the condition of the contents can be observed. In the final test all dark eggs or those showing spots or black shadows should be rejected. 8. The best months for preserving are March, April, May and June. It has been found in many cases that summer eggs do not keep nearly so well as those laid before the hot weather. 9. Preserved eggs should be sold under that name, and not as "new-/aids," "breakfast," or " fresh" eggs.—Boa-rd of Agriculture Leaflet 8.

THE USE OF DOGS IN WAR.

An article on "War Duties for the Dog," which appears in the June number of they "Windsor Magazine," ihow s how largely the use of dogs has been developed during the war for dispatch work, the tracing of the wounded. sentry duties, and various other activitiei, and no dog-lover should miss the photographs which give authority to the writer's records of the re- markable intelligence to which the dog has been trained .for, war service by all the principal 'Combatants in the - war. The writer, Mr. Ignatius Phayre, has some interesting points as to the various breeds and cross-breeds which have proved the most useful. In the course of his theme he says:— " General Sir Charles Tucker recommended their use, after seeing the marvels they wrought with the Italian Alpini under Captain Cistola, and also under Captain Persidski—of Count Keller's staff—in the Manchurian campaign' Captain Persidski reports as follow: 'ln find- ing the wounded and missing with which the millet fields were strewn, no agency succeeded like our dogs. The British dogs are especiallY clever, and in a , recent skirmish recorered twenty-three fallen men in the most unlikelY places.'"

CHOIR FESTIVAL.

Music Excellently Rendered At Legh

Street Primitive.

The choir festival in connection with Legh- ttreet Primitive Methodist Church was held t,n Sunday, when large congregations assembled at eaCh service.

The preacher for the morning was the Rev. A. J. Chessum, who delivered an interesting Sermon, giving instances of how the power of solig had been an influence for good to 'man- kind. The anthem "Call to remembrance," vas rendered by the choir, the opening solo being taken by Mr. J. Phillips, while Mr. 3. '1 -)akin gave the solo, "Christie Salvator.". In the afternoon the service was presided over by Mr. F. S. Bridge, of Liverpool, who was once a scholar in the Sunday school at Legh-street, a member of the choir and, eventually, organist. In the course of some recollections he Mentioned that once a choir practice at the chapel was called for six o'clock in the morning. Ilefore proceeding with the cantata entitled •" Daughter of Jairus " (Stainer), the Rev. A. a- Chessum gave a brief exposition on the words of the same. The soprano solos were taken in a pleasing and artistic manner by Mrs. C. NVorthington, who also took part in the beauti- ful and popular duet "Love Divine." Mr. T. Goostrey sang the tenor part, the voices blending exceedingly well. Mr. T. Goostrey also gave the solo " My Hope is in the Everlasting," his .xendition being full of true pathos and feeling. Dakin, who possesses a-bass voice of good quality, was responsible for the bass solos, while the three principals were at an advantage in the obligato trio in the closing chorus. An exceptionally pleasing feature of the after- noon was the beautiful rendering of the female chorus in four parts, "Sweet Gentle Flower," the voices blending to perfection. The preachlr at the evening service was Mr. John Upson. The choir gave a good interpretation of the chorus, "We Never Will Bow Down." Miss Maud Jones was very successful in her vocal- ising of the solo, "Father of Heaven," each of the above pieces being selections from Handel's " Judas Maccabaeus." Mr. J. Dakin sang " Honour and Arms." Called upon at short notice owing to the Tegular organist of the church, Mr. F. 11. aanner, having recently joined the Colours, Mr. 'l'. Tanner acted as organist in an able manner. kr. A. Spruce conducted the chorus. The collections in aid of choir funds were a considerable advance on previous years.

HAPPY WARRIORS.

Will The Message Of The Church

Survive The Fall 01 The Nations?

Next October a National Mission is to take Plate, and the clergy of Warrington have arranged a series of devotional services for the last Saturday in each month in preparation for the mission. The second of these preparatory services took Place at St. Paul's Claurth last Saturday, the itev. E. Downham officiating. Prayers were effered on behalf of the Church, clergy and zation.

HALF CENTURY OF SERVICE.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
Necessity Turned To Gain.

Th. Rev. E. DOwnham gave an address from Lhe test, "Preach the word of God in season." Wordsworth, said the reverend gentleman, hail a very fine poem, "The Character of the Happy Warrior." The poet asked the question, "Who Ifs the happy warrior?" and the reply was he

"Who, doomed to go in company with pain, And fear, and bloodshed, miserable train! Turns his necessity to glorious gain."

To the warrior, the bloodshed and the fear and Vain were a necessity. He could not change them, but, by his bravery, his faithfulness to •duty, and his sacrifice, he turned necessity into Rlorious gain. What striking examples we had lad of this happy warrior during the war: Men Who had turned necessity into glorious gain. To them the battle and the fighting were a neces- sity; but behold the glorious gain--the bravery, devotion and self-sacrifice. Tho war was our necessity; we could not alter it What we required were "happy warriors," Who would "preach the word of God in season," and turn this necessity into glorious gain for God and the nation. A whole of civil- isation seemed to be in ruins. Men in their bewilderment were crying out, "What must we Ida to be saved?" Had the Church got a message for men, for the nation, a message that was 'Divine, experimental, that would survive the crash of systems and the fall of nations? We believed that we had such a message in the %spa of Jesus Christ. What we needed were " happy warriors," who would have faith in that message which alone would turn this necessity—the war—into glorious gain f4:r acd and man.

Fello-w-ing the address, special war litany was said.

Among those present were the Rector (Rev. vanon Willis), who conducted the first service of the series at the Parish Church in April, 11_11d the Revs. B. Percival, J. Davies, A-. M. Mitchell and J. Martin.

Mr. Elijah Place's Death After Fifty Years In Market.

CORPORATION TRIBUTE.

In the death of Mr. Elijah Place, on Monday morning, at his residence, 27, Stanley-street, Warrington has lost its oldest official; also one of its most highly respected. Mr. Place was in his eighty-first year, and for nearly 50 years enjoyed the confidence of the townspeople in the post of Superintendent of Markets, and Chief Inspector of Weights and Measures. Until about 12 months ago, Mr. Place was able to carry out his duties with assiduity, but breakdown in health reduced him to a state of invalidity, and only occasionally was he seen in the markets in recent months. Yfis interest in them, however, was maintained until within a few hours of his death, which, despite his weak condition, came rather unexpectedly. Mr. Place hailed from the Darwen districr, where most of his relatives have resided for many generations. He came to Warrington fifty-two years ago, and within two years of ser- vice under the Corporation,. gained their confi- dence to appoint him to the positions which he held throughout the remainder of his life. In the development and construction of the

===== 0002601_19160603_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE MR. E. PLACE.

markets Mr. Place has played his part, awl, happily, has usually had a sympathetic com- mittee, who appreciated improvements he sug- gested. When he first took charge of the markets they were confined to the old Markat Hall, with a large number of stalls forming a long tail from the steps almost down to the bottom of Market-street. Inconvenience to stall- holders and customers led to the establishmeitt of the splendid market now open, and Mr. Place had no small measure of credit in its development. While enjoying the confidence of the committee, he was also esteemed by the stall- holders, who, as he grew into advanced years, affectionately called him "Pa."

Mr. Place was an ardent Wesleyan. When he first came to Warrington he and his wife at- tended the church in Bold-street for a number f.f years, and then went to Bewsey-road. Leaving there a few years ago, when their habit of regu- larly attending the services proved too great a tax on their strength, they, returned to .Bold- street, that church .being much nearer to thew home. For a great number of years Mr. Place was a trustee of the church in Bewsey-road, but resigned on discontinuing worshipping there. When not on duty during the week, he de- voted most of his time to recreation out of doors. He was a keen lover of fresh air, a strenuous long-distance walker, an enthusiastic angler. and a capable handler of the woods. In his long association with the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club he won many prizes on the bowling green, and, though in recent years he was less adept, it was always obvious that he had been a formidable exponent of the game. In his domestic life Mr. Place was well favoured. He had two sons and five daughters, and the first break in the family occurred in January of this year, when one of the daughters,' Helena, Mrs. Maitland, passed away. • One son,' John, is a fleet paymaster, the other, Charles, is chief weights and measures inspector at Bradford. Miss Annie Place is headmistress at the Rishton Wesleyan Infants' School, and Miss Amy is head of the girls' department at Bolton Council School. THE FUNERAL.

The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Thursday, being preceded by a service in Bold- street Weeleyan Church, conducted by the Rev. W. T. Derry. Besides members of the family there were present :—The Mayor (Alderman

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY. JUNE 3, 1916.

WOMEN'S WAR BADGES.

Official Recognition For Volun- tary Workers On Comforts.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
LADY CROSFIELD'S CON- GRATULATIONS.

While men are •thrusting bayonets, tU Liberal women of Warrington are brandishing needles and, not being . within, range for effectively prodding Germans, delight in mak- ing the best use of them by manufacturing in- dispensable comforts for our warriors.

About 300 of these ladies assembled in the John Crosfield Memorial Hall, on Wednesday evening, and 'received bronze badges signifying that they are war workers. Mrs. Osborne, the hon. secretary, remarked that some of them deserved gold medals, but this was not to dis- parage the gifts, but to emphasise vividly the strenuous work which several especially have done. "In some cases," she remarked with pride, "you have turned out from 80 to 100 shirts and several hundred socks each. Every one has done according to the time at her dis- posal and, while giving great praise to those who have done the most, we feel just as proud of those who have done a smaller share, and we all hope you will continue to do your part, for we still need war comforts in large measure, and if we keep working steadily the brave men at the Front will be well looked after." (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19160603_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
Badges To Be Proud Of.

As becomes adornments far ladies, th.) badges are good-looking, a composite V.W. (Voluntary Workers) in bronze being topped by a crown of the same metal. What gives them value, however, is that they are official. As Mrs. Osborne explained, some time ago the members of- the Aseociation- were asked by the Government to join the Voluntary Organisa- tion Scheme in order to save overlapping and wastage, aild to supply comforts in a systematic manner. Some Associations in Warrington con- tinued to hold aloof, but the following had be- come affiliated: The Women's Liberal Ahsocia- tion, the Territorial Equipment Fund Distribut- ing Committee, and the Cairo-street Working Party.

For joining the scheme, Mrs. Osborne said, they were now having their reward by receiving badges of which they could well be prondc (Applause.)

Being sent by the Government; the badges had to have a formal reception, each worker having to sign the following form: "I, being a regular voluntary worker under the Director-General of Voluntary Organisation, and having completed three months' service under the department, acknowledge the receipt of a voluntary worker's badge, which I under- take to return to the Association of which I am a registered member, on ceasing to be a regular worker, and under no other circumstances what- ever will I part with the badge." Then followed the signature of the receiver and her address. The number on the paper is duplicated on the back of the badge. Lady Crosfield Praises Women Workers.

During her speech Mrs. Osborne read the following interesting letter from Lady Crosfield, it being punctuated with cheers as it was given out :

"I am writing to ask you to convey to the members of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association warmest and most'sincere congratu- lations from Sir Arthur Crosfield and myself on the fact that to-morrow evening they will receive such well-merited recognition from the Government for the splendid work they have done, and which I am very sure they intend to continue until the end of this dreadful war. "Among many miscalculations which misled the Huns at the time they took their desperate and fatal plunge, the most serious of all was their conviction that England could not, and would not, enter into the war; and another great mistake they made was the idea they had formed of the English as a decadent race. The splendid and patriotic courage shown, the services rendered and sacrifices made by our soldiers in the field, and the millions who have voluntarily gone to participate in that heroic task constitutes an answer to this impudent opinion formed by the Huns, and at the same time teaches them the severest and sternest lesson they have ever had to learn. "The splendid way in which the women of England have rallied to the national cause during these terrible times has, in the same way, helped to teach our barbarous enemy the same much-needed lesson. In our factories and fields, our offices and workshops, they have rendered services of priceless value to the old country, services without which our statesmen and soldiers would have utterly failed to grapple with the colossal task with which they have been, and still are, faced. And surely of all the work so splendidly performed by the women of England few things appeal to us so much as the unselfish zeal with which such large numbers of our women, after a hard day's work at home or elsewhere, have, week after week, devoted their well-earned leisure towards Producing articles of clothing to safeguard the health and add to the comfort of our lads at the Front. Shirts, socks, mufflers, mittens, &c., &R., are more than comforts—they are Positive necessities—and both Sir Arthur and I earnestly hope, therefore, that the admirable efforts of the Liberal women of Warrington will be continued with unflagging energy until the time comes to ring down the curtain on this fearful European tragedy.",„

===== 0002601_19160603_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
Go On Until The End.

Miss Bessie Dutton was among those present and, on the invitation of Mrs. Osborne, addressed the meeting. Besides being an objet of pride and satisfaction, she said, the badges would be an example to others. They showed that the wearers were denying themselves by doing their duty to their country, and she was sure they intended doing that until the end of the war. (Applause.) The distribution of the badges then began. Tables with two clerical ladies at each were arranged in four or five parts of the hall,' and members came and signed the necessary receipt forms as if they were attesting under the group system.

LONG AND FAITHFUL SERVICE.

At a recent meeting of the General Purposes Committee of the Warrington Town Council Mr. Place's death was reported, and it was re- solved :—" That this committee place on record an acknowledgment of his long and faithful ser- vice with the Corporation, extending over a period of 50 years, and an-appreciation of-M3- upright character; and that the Town Clerk convey to the family of the late Mr. Place an expression of regret and the condolence of this committee with them in their bereavement."

o+oooooooooooooooooo+ooooooooooooooooooo 000+00 0 00000+000000000000000000000000000000000000 000 0 0 00 00 0+ f.•"•: ••• WOMAN'S WORLD. u o.:. CHAT and GOSSIP. 'Summer Muslin Frocks.

when the help of every individual may be turned to good account.

Provided the weather is hot and fine, muslin dresses will be almost universally worn, for they lend themselves especially to the present Early Victorian modes. The soft clinging materials that were necessary to the narrow silhouette,, have given place to fabrics that stand out of themselves, hence the revival of the alpaca and mohair. Thee muslins. of this year-are also.no longer of soft silk, but of crisp, almost stiff cotton. Plain white muslin will be much in evidence, although in some instances a touch of colour is given to it by means of a motif embroidered in delicate pastel shades. The daintiest floral patterns, on a white muslin ground are also shown, and these are made with full skirts flounced or gauged, and finished with a sash to match the flowers in colour. In black, white and coloured stripes and spots, in grey, black, and other plain but gay colours, these. mnslins will make the most charming of sum- mer gowns, finished with white collars. Nuns veiling, mousseline de laine, and silk jersey cloth, will also be very much in evidence this summer, for simple, inexpensive frocks.

Floral Motifs on Summer Garments. Never perhaps has handwork, or its imitations been more extensively used on summer blouses, coatfrocks, afternoon, and other dresses. The all-white blouse seems to have lost favour, for it is generally given some kind of dainty floral decoration, except when worn with an all-white skirt. Simply-made white blouses have motifs of tiny flowers, or miniature fruit, either worked in coloured floss silk, or simply outlined in buttonhole stitch, to give contrast and relief. The narrowest ribbon embroidery forms another effective decoration for a white blouse. Such floral motifs are quite a feature of this season's trimmings, and are to be seen in worsteds as well as in washing silks and cotton on dresses for smart wear, and on pockets, lapels, and cuffs of summer wrap coats of faille and taffetas. Belts also are thus decorated. Designs are both natural and conventional, and sometimes tips, latter are given Futurist colourings, but in ail cases the flowers and fruit are small, even tiny, and generally worked by hand, which adds naturally to the expense and exclusiveness ,if the garments they adorn.

Egg Potato. Egg potato is very simple and very good. cold boiled potatoes am cut into rather small pieces, and moistened with soup stock and cream. On a layer of potatoes is laid a layer of sliced hard- boiled egg, then seasoning of salt, pepper, parsley, or shallot, and bits of butter, then another layer of potatoes, alternating in this manner through the dish, with the egg for 013 top layer, sprinkled with breadcrumbs and butter and cream. It is baked for half to three- quarters of an hour, or for an hour if in a deep dish of earthenware.

By MARJORIE.

Boycotting German Trade. The Overseas Dominions, or as they were formerly called, the Colonies, have often set the Mother Country a good example. This is notably the case in the action of the women of Melbourne, which should serve as an object lesson to every woman in these islands. A meet- irig was lately held in Melbourne, and attended by thousands of the gentler sex, includ- ing the wives and daughters of all the prominent citizens, with the laud- able object of bringing about a boycott of German goods in one, if not all the provin,!es of Australia. Every woman present undertook to refuse absolutely from that day, to purchase enemy goods. They also agreed to give the pre- ference to British productions, but if these were not forthcoming, to buy only those made by the Allies. The Welfare of the Babies.

The public conscience has lately awakened to the necessity of preserving infant life, which is sacrificed as much from ignorance as from heartlessness. Organisations work primarily for the welfare of the child as well as for assisting and advising mothers, and all have to depend almost entirely on voluntary support. Every day the cry for further help becomes more in- sistent, for although London has over eighty day nurseries there is room for many more. Thirty- eight of these are affiliated to the National Society of Day Nurseries, which inspects creches, founds new ones, and aims at securing the best possible treatment of the babies left in the creches. When funds permit the society gives grants, but its main work is advisory. It employs a lady inspector, who visits the nur- series every day. Children are taken from one month, Until they are five years old. The war htls considerably increased the necessity for these creches, as so many mothers are now employed in munition factories. Women Signallers' Good Work.

Lady Glanusk, the honorary president of the Women Signallers' Territorial Corps, lately presented that useful and patriotic organisation with a very handsome standard. The demand for trained signallers, as qualified instructors for the Army, is at the present moment most urgent, as so many of the men hitherto employed have gone to the Front. Members of the W.S.T.C. are doing good work in the Army. More members are needed to train in order that when qualified they may teach others. Signal- ling has been described as the nerves of an army, to which it is of vital importance, as well as to a country threatened with invasion. It is a work that educated women with leisure to devote to it, can do for the country at a time

HOW TO OBSERVE. WALKING DAY.

My Dear Children,— Not all my 800 nephews and nieces have written to say how they think Walking Day should be observed ,this year but, as you will see, a very fair number of them have done so, and I have no doubt that the opinions ex- pressed in the Corner will be well considered by the authorities of the town and the schools when they are making their arrangements. There were quite a dozen letters that deserved a prize, but I have had to be content by giving two— both first prizes, as I think the letters were iq equal value. The first prize goes to Niece Ethel Shaw, Sunnyside, Chester New-road, No. 533, who at- tends Bolton Council School, and is twelve years of age. The other first prize goes to Nephew Ernest William Sankey, 99, Kimberley-street, who attends St. Barnabas' School, and is only ten years of age. You have now had a fortnight's experience of "daylight saving" as they call it. Of course, we really mean that we use day- light and save gas, which is a very sensible thing to do, seeing that we get a good natural light for nothing, while the poor imitation is very expensive. I should like to know what you think of the new practice, so 1 will give the prize next week for an essay on Daylight Saving.

You will be glad to know that I have had an interesting letter from Uncle Ned this week. He is delighted with his ,numerous family of nephews and nieces; and he will be happy to know that in many of the letters I receive he is remembered by our little folk. I know he would like the war to end, if only to get back to his Cornet.

Your loving, UNCLE JACK.

THE WINNING LETTERS.

By Niece Ethel Shaw, Sunnyside, Chester New-road; Bolton Council School; No. 533; age 12:—

"Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sure every boy and girl will be overjoyed to hear that there is to be a Walking Day this year; and yet there may be some who are not inclined to be this way, for they may be thinking of loved• ones, who in former years have been in the Walking Day field amusing them, and-have now been called to arms to help to win the war which is now taking place. "So I think we ought to spend Walking Day a little quieter and more thoughtfully this year than in previous years, and at the same time we ought to be very grateful to the soldiers at the Front whose wish it was that there should be a Walking Day." By Nephew Ernest William Sankey, 99, Kim- berley-street; St. Barnabas' School; No. 766; age 10: _ _ _

"We should all observe Walking Day this year with the keenest interest. It is better. to have a Walking Day than to do witlwut one, as it encourages boys and girls to go to Sunday school on Sunday afternoon instead of spending the afternoon playing or going walks. It will cost a. lot of money to pay for-the food, tea and the loan of the field, considering the price of things, but if the boys and girls bring a copper or two and the people who take charge of the money buy the cheapest things it will all go well. We ought te thank the soldiers for asking why we did not have one last year, and the men in authority for taking the matter into their hands. Every parish is having a Walking Day, but cannot go out of town because the train fare is too much, and there are no excursion trains. It will not make much difference to the children, as they will walk round the town in the morning, and walk to the field and have races and tea in the afternoon. Ido not suppose that the prizes will cost as much as last year, be- cause nearly all the toys that came into Eng- land two or three years ago all came from Ger- many, and there is not much tin, steel and other material used in the making of toys left in England, and none i .ie s being brought in because the ships are being used to transport soldiers, guns, rifles and food to the countries where the British are fighting."

MUST WALKING DAY BE POSTPONED?

We believe that in the interests of the nation, our annual children's holiday must be postponed. The call of the Government is plain and unequivocal. Munitions are badly wanted at the Front. The long tragedy at Verdun is still, after more than a hundred days of continued and ,unequalled slaughter, unfinished. It has, however, reached the crisis, and before long it must either achieve its end or die out. We have such confidence in our glorious French Allies that we. believe this greatett act of the war will end in Ger- man defeat; but, whichever way it ends, the danger to our own men is the same. If the Germans get through towards Paris on the Verdun side, they will turn viciously Upon us and try to rend us on the Ypres side; if they are beaten they will still, beast-like, dash themselves upon us with the fury of despair. We are officially told that the holidays taken at Easter diminished our supply of munitions by an amount equal to the material used up at Loos. Can -we, in such circumstances, afford to run the risk of reducing our output of the means whereby we can scotch the foe and save the lives of our heroes at the Front?

We know we have championed the holding of Walking Day this year, for the sake of the children. Soldiers write from the Front say- ing they rejoice in the fact that the kiddies are not to be robbed of -their...pleasures. , The, children, it is argued, may have a curtailed

Wit Ming Day, and the works may keep on Working just the same. But would they? Walking Day calls, for the.help of many adults and the older boys and girls are now on war work playing the part their absent fathers and brothers would be playing had they not been. called to the Colours. Always ' the children's parents take a hand in the festival. Walking Day in any form would be a great holiday, and the War Office. have asked the nation to kill the holiday spirit lead for two months. We have made inquiries at • the works in the town and there appears to be a division of opinion as to whether the Walk- ing Day would this year interfere with the work of the men. Of .course, no one can give a decided opinion; but why should we run any risk at this vitally critical juncture?

, Walking Day is not a town a'ffair, but an affair of ,the Sunday Schools. MightY we sug- gest that the 'managers of all the schools should meet—and meet quickly to avoid going to any expense that may be wasted---iind decide .upen -whether they will unite in post- poning Walking Day? We understand that the Roman Catholic cicrgy are strongly in favour of a postponement, and the Anglican and the Free Churches will agree to do any- thing that tells for the safety of our Armies old the security of the country. But, as we have said, they must act, .and act promptly. A half and half' Walking Day would have all the dangers of a proper Walking Day with only a tithe of its advantages.

NO WHIT HOLIDAYS.

If the reasons for not observing Walking Day are so strong, they are ,incalculably stronger in respect to Whit-week. The banks will be onened, work will proceed' in the fac- tories, and, we presume, the schools will, con- tinue to instruct, because the Board of Educa- tion are issuing 'to education authorities a circular advising them to arrange the holi- days for children in keeping with the holidays of the parents_which have been put back to August. The Town Clerk advises us that locally the Municipal Offices will remain open. Tradesmen in town- appear to be unanimously in favour of Opexiing their shops on Whit Monday, and altogether we seem well content to keep down the holiday spirit over an acute period in the course of the war, and a pre- eminently critical point in the. history of out race. It. should .be borne in mind that neither the abandonment of Walking Day nor of Whit Monday is suggested. The holidays are merely postponed till the threatening clouds shall have passed away. Those at the helm suggest that the clouds will have dis- persed by the beginning of August, for the Tuesday after August Bank Holiday is to be observed as Whit Monday. We could have Walking Day at the same time, and a delight- ful Walking Day it would be if the clouds had really rolled away!

===== 0002601_19160603_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EARLIER CLOSING OF SHOPS.

The Tradesmen's Association took a lead- ing Dart in the effort which recently resulted in the closing of the Markets at ten o'clock on Saturday .nights.. The majority. of trades- men readily fell into line and observed the undertaking to close not later than the hour at which the Markets Closed; but there have been some abstensions, and there is a fear that 'the. Markets Coni•Mittee. will rescind the new bylaw unless uniformity of action can be achieved. We see that the matter has been before the Markets Committee, and that the Tradesmen's Association have assured the town authorities That they will endeavour to obtain uniforinity, and in the event of failure will endeavour tó obtain. an Order under the Shops Act, 1912. In the meantime we are asked to state that the Tradesmen's Associa- tion very respectfully ask the general public to do all their shopping before 10 p.m. on Saturdays, so that some relief will. be .given not only to the tradesmen, but to the large .body of lady assistants who have replaced the male assistants who have. been Called to the Colours. 'Early closing saves gas and elec- tricity,' and so helps to win the war, which. is even more important than acting humanely to the shop assistants.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
GO:3IP.

With fine weather the garden party in Bank Park this afternoon should be an immense success.

• The- attendance is certainly going to be im- mense, for Went 1,800 wives and widows of soldiers and sailors have accepted the invitation of the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Peter Peacock).

The afternoon's reception is to take place on the lawn at the rear of the Town Hall. The ladies need not fear of .being tired, for the Parr Hall has been denuded of forms for their comfort.

The refreshments will be a very agreeable interlude, about fifty ladies having promised to wait upon them as they sit at ease.

If it gets hot there will be a demand for ices. A large quantity of ice-cream has been ordered, so they will be all right in that direction. When all of it has gone they will have to rely on their parasols to keep cool.

The Mayor and Mayoress have invited the company to dance on the lawn in front of the Town Hall from 7.30 to 9.30. The band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment is to play, and the Scarlet Poms will be in the neighbourhood to entertain those of less robust and agile consti- tutions.

Really. the .garden party will develop into a fete. May we have 'fine weather!

The Watch Committee have agreed to join in representations to be made to the Home Secre- tary in favour of the establishment of a Government Censorship of films of a harmful character that are shown at cinematograph exhibitions.

Mr. W. It. Woodcock, the Borough Collector, provides us every half-year with figures that ought to make us all optimists. He and his staff collected for the half-year ended March 31st, no less than £80,872, and only £22 12s. was lost by non-payment. Arrears carried forward were 2107 19s.

'During the year ended March 31st, the gross receipts on the tramcars was 0,000, and the net profit £2,600.

On the motor 'bus service there was a net loss of £396, against a loss last year of £554.

More Pensions Committee "fuss." At the last meeting of the General Purposes Committee it was resolved "That the resignation of Councillor Dickenson as a member of the Naval and Military War Pensions Committe, be accepted," and the Mayor ei-as appointed in his stead.

. Itvras. retorted .thitt;.Mr:llale had enlisted and -that the Trades Unions had Trades Council

had been- requested to recommend a member in his stead. It was also reported that the namcs of Messrs. Dukes and Seabury had been again recommended for appointment, and the com- mittee resolved "That such recommendation be not entertained." That sounds decisive.

Alderman Smethurst has been added to the Pensions Committee on 'the -recommendation of the Lancashire Territorial Association.

A word may here be said for the organisers of the Hippodrome matinee in aid of blinded soldiers and sailors. Mrs. Henry Greenall, Mr. W. A. Jackson and Mr. J. H. J. Hatnpson (hon. secretary of the Working Men's Infirmary Com- mittee) did yeoman service working untiringly for its success. They had a splendid cause and, to use an American phrase, they understood how to "push the goods."-

An undertaking of this magnitude wants some doing. And they did it. An enthusiastic band of helpers rallied round, and the funds will be all the better for the 5320 realised.

I was glad to, hear the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) was, so emphatic in his compliments..

The Daylight Saving Bill has had a marked effect -upon the consumption of electricity and gas, but I have• been unable to obtain,*the figures in the former. commodity. The gas de- partment receipts, however, were down by about two per cent. over the correspondnig week last year; and a calculation on this basis shows that the revenue will be the poorer by about £1,300 throughout the year.

It is urged by the Government that the public should 'diminish their consumption of electricity and gas, primarily with the object of saving coaLand labour.

"THE DIAMOND FROM THE SKY."

Prize Story That Begins In "The Examiner" To-day.

FILM PRODUCTION NEXT WEEK.

"The Diamond From the Sky," claimel to be one of the most powerful stories ever produceti, is to run in serial form for about 30 weeks at two cinema houses, the Pavilion, Lovely-lane, and the Grand Electric Theatre, Bridge Foot. It is a £2,000 prize story. The story deals with a diamond, which came to earth in America between two and three centuries ago, through the medium of a meteor, and saved the life of an Englishman who stcod in peril of instant death at the hands of the Indian natives. The Englishman adopted the stone as a family talisman. The experiences of this talisman is the centre of the story. ' Incident crowds upon incident. The plotting and scheming of some of the characters is masterful in the extreme, the troubles of the beautiful heroine and the humorous Passages making complete a most wonderful conception. A novelty is provided for Warringtonians in connection with this story. Each week an in- stalment will appear in "The Examiner," giving in full the details of the portion of the story that is to be filmed in town during the following week, so that patrons of the two hou.ses concerned will be able to follow the plot in- telligently and clearly. _

The first instalment of "The Diamond From the Sky" appears in to-day's " Examiner " on. page 7, dealing exhaustively with next week's pictures. The proprietors of The Grand will admit you at a reduced charge if you will cut out their advertisement, ; which appears in to-day's "Examiner," and present it on paying for admission.

Do not miss the opening chapter, whieh appears on page 7.
