FIRE AT COCKHEDGE.

Nocturna Outbreak Quelled By Two Brigades.

Just before half-past ten o'clock on Wednes- day night the "buzzer" at Cockhedge Mills drew attention with a penetrating screech to a fire which had broken out there, and soon the Works' Fire Brigade and the Borough Brigade were on the scene and, engaged in quelling ,an outbreak in the large weaving shed and ware- house. The whole place was thick with smoke and -difficulty was. experienced in gaining a .way to the locality of the fire. After strenuous work for an hour and a half the conflagration was quelled. The damage is inconsiderable, thanks to the prompt action of the brigades. It is presumed that the fire originated through the over-heating of a shearing machine. An ever-swelling crowd assembled, but saw nothing flaming- and picturesque. Happily, the fire ended in smoke. It is, interesting, to note that two brothers were in charge of the respective Fire Brigades —Superintendent Frank Turner, of the Borough Brigade, and Superintendent Thomas Turner, of the Works'.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
COMING TO-DAY.

Visit Of Naval Expert To Royal

Court Theatre.

Fre,h from his visit to the Grand Fleet, Mr. A. If. Pollen, the famous naval expert, is to visit the Royal Court Theatre this (Saturday) afternoon, to deliver a lecture on his experiences with, and his observations of the Navy. Before the war began Mr. Pollen had a high reputation which has since been increased, his insight into and knowledge of the Navy and its doings have been of great value in enlightening public opinion and helping civilians to form an adequate and sensible view of the naval situation. Mr. Pollen may really claim to be an expert. For twelve years his system of fire control and theories of gunnery were the special secret of the British Admiralty, and many are the compliments paid him by the high officers of the Senior Service. Mr. Pollen is an excellent platform speaker, and everyone who hears him this (Saturday) afternoon will be both interested and instructed.

Births and Deaths.

During the week ended May 27th, there were 30 births in Warrington—fifteen "males an 3 fifteen . females. The deaths numbered 23— twelve males and eleven females. In the corre- sponding week last year there. were 42 births and 10 deaths.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Bronchitis and Pneumonia.

Eight of the 23 deaths in Warrington last week resulted from bronchitis and pneumonia— four of each—and there was one death from each of the following causes: Phthisis, other tuberculdus disease, cancer, premature birth, heart disease. All other causes numbered ten.

The Medical Officer of Health (Dr. G. W. N. The Bishop of Chester had been announced to preach at St. James' (Latchford) Church on the evening of Ascension Day -(Thursday), and a large congregation was present to hear him, hut were disappointed to learn from the Vicar (the Rev. Walking Sticks for the Wounded.

Daisy Maim, a young lady Member of the "Pansy League," living at Clacton, has shown her sympathy for our wounded soldiers by sending 12s. 6d. to Mr. Royston's fund for sup- plying walking sticks to wounded soldiers, and tells how she raised this splendid sum, and she should be prouder still if she stirs up others to imitate her.

She says: "I have collected for it and worked for it in various ways, i.e., making blotters, housewives and pincushions, cleaning boots for id. a pair, washing pocket handkerchiefs at six a penny, and ironing ties, till at, last I collected 12s. 6d., Which I hope will buy a good many sticks for our brave wounded soldiers.

Diligent Guardians. The Year Book of the Warrington Union and Rural District Council for 1916-17 has been issued and, as usual, reflects the greatest credit upon the officials who have compiled it.

In an accompanying list of attendances of Guardians for the last complete year it is shown how closely many members attend to their duties. Altogether 284 meetings were held dur- ing the year. Mr. A. J. Jolley, the chairman, who this year completes quarter of a century of Poor Law work, attended 26 board meetings out of a possible 26; House Committee meetings, 22 out of 25; Finance Committee, 23 out of 25; Relief Committee, 27 out of 27; Assessment Committee. 16 out of 17; Farm Committee, four out of six; Building Committee, the only meet- ing held; Boarding-out Committee, six out of six; and Military Committee, 45 out of 45. There are no other records quite so good os this, but Mr. Furness, Mr. Brooks, Mr. Billinge, Miss Broadbent, Mr. Mather, Rev. A. M. Mitchell, Mrs. 'Hall and Mr. Hughes Ire among some who were very regular in their attendances.

Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Anniversary ...t4O. 4s. 7a. was the total collection at the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Sunday School Anni- versary held on Sunday morning in the adjoining church and in the afternoon and evening in Parr Hall. The well-known evangelist, the Rev. Thomas Waugh, was the preacher in the morning and evening, and his earnest discourses had an impressive effect. The music, which was rendered under the conduc- torship of Mr. Levi Richardson, was of a high quality, particularly so at .the musical service in the afternoon. Alderman Bennett presided and Mr. John Upson delivered an interesting address. Solos were sung by Miss. G. Bennett, Mrs. A. Jones, Miss Bromley, Miss Pollie Gough and Miss Moores. A -quartet was given by Eva Pennington, Ruth Cowdell, Harold J. Blythe and Ernest Beddard. Miss Rawlings recited two hymns, and Mr. T. Kaye took the solo in the anthem "Jerusalem"; Mrs. Barber, of Altrincham, sang as a solo "Angels ever bright and fair" in the afternoon, and "With verdure clad" in the evening. Miss May Isherwood also gave as a. solo "Nearer, my God, to Thee." Miss G. Atherton acted as accompanist.

Butcher's Bankruptcy. At the Bankruptcy Court yesterday, Thomas Warburton, butcher, formerly carrying on busi- ness at 69, Buttermarket-street, appeared for his public examination in bankruptcy. Debtor stated that he commenced business On his own account in 1908 with £6O capital, the extent of his savings, at 98, Buttermarket-street. The takings at first were about £2B weekly, but subsequently increased to £llO, including wholesale and retail trade. His retail trade in- creased from £2B to £55. Two Tears later the takings dropped to £6 weekly as the result of a prosecution at the Police Court, and he never quite recovered the ground he had lost: From Septembers, 1911, until twelve months later, he was ill and was unable to do any work during that time. In his absence the business was carried on by two of his brothers. He closed the business, and after paying all debts, he had a balance of about £4O. When sufficiently re- covered from his illness he again started in busi- ness at 69, Buttermarket-street, but it was not. so successful. The liabilities amounted to £433 7s. 10d., and assets were expected to valise £23 ss. 9d.., leaving a deficiency of £3B 2s. id., ma le up as follows: Net loss on trade, £B6 28. id.; domestic expenses, wife and one child, £225; medical attendance and cost of illness, £50;•lois 1 on cattle, £3O. In reply to Mr. A. Browne, who appeared for debtor, witness said that part of his failure was due to signing a hire agreement for a sausage machine for £55 3s. 4d., for which he had paid £2l on account. The balance was included in the liabilities for which he had had no benefit. The examination WAS closed.

Hoyle will address the chil- dren of Cairo-street in the afternoon on " Grenfel, of Labrador." Child Burned to Death.

A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned at an inquest held at the Police Station yester- day, on the body of Elizabeth Crump, aged one year and eight months, daughter of William arid Elizabeth Crump, 9, Brook-street. It was stated that Mrs. Crump, who is the mother of six chil- dren, and whose husband, is serving in Egypt, left home as usual at 7.25 on Wednesday, the 31st ult., in order to go, to work. A woman whom she employed to look after the children- had occasion to go next door to get some hot water for the tea, when she heard one of the children shout. She returned immediately, and found deceased in flames, which she put out with her skirt, but the girl died at the Infirmary on Thursday. There was a gas ring in the parlour attached to the gas bracket, and this had been used by the woman, and afterwards by a boy, but both were certain they turned the gas cut after use.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Death of Alderman Burton's Eldest

Son.

We regret to announce the death of Mr. William Burton, eldest son of Alderman Burton, of Stafford-road, Stockton Heath, fol- lowing- heart failure,. at his residence, Newton- road, Lowton, on Wednesday afternoon. Mr. Burton was 50 years of ;Ewe, and for 26 years had held the position of head master of Lowton Schools. He was of a very patriotic disposition, and immediately upon the outbreak of wa? offered his services and was accepted as a member of the Civic Guard. Whilst ardu- ously performing his duties he contracted pneu- monia and pleurisy, which left him in a very weak condition, and death supervened. He was a well-known botanist and has delivered several lectures in Warrington. He leaves eight chil- dren, three of whom are serving at the Front. One of them enlisted at the age of 16 years and has been on active service over twelve months, while another was in the retreat from Mons. The funeral is to take place to-day (Satur- day) at Lowton, and it is a sad coincidence that his eldest daughter was to have been married to-day. Elderly Invalid Found Drowned. •

said he would rather take away his life than go back ,to the Workhouse," was a recol- lection of Mrs. Samuel Tighe, at an inquest at the Police Station, on Wednesday afternoon, concerning the death of her husband, a lab- ourer, who had resided with her at 3, Smith's- court, Winwick-road, and who was found drowned in the St. Helens and Sankey Canal on Monday. She added that her husband was- -62 years of age, and, until January last, enjoyed good health. He then had a stroke, and a fortnight later was taken to the Workhouse Hospital. She visited him repeatedly and he always implored her to take him home. - This she did in the Middle of April. He seemed to get on all right during the first week, and then he went queer. She wanted him to go back to the Hospital, and it was when she urged this course upon him that he vented the, sentiments given at the opening of this para- graph.. Mrs. Tighe added that her husband. went for a walk on Monday morning and the next time she saw him was in the mortuary.— Verdict "Found drowned, there being no evi- dence as to how he got into the water." •

===== 0002601_19160603_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
New Offices for the Insurance Committee.

The Warrington Insurance Committee, t their meeting on Thursday evening, approved of a recommendation of the Finance Committee that the rooms in Horsemarket-street Chambers, now occupied by the Warrington Junior Liberal Association, be taken over as offices on a seven years' lease, at a rental of £35 per annum, plus rates and taxes, the Insurance Committee to be held responsible for any alterations required. Negotiations in the matter had been conducted by the Mayor and the Chairrhan of the Finance Committee, Mr. S. Brocklehurst. Their recom- mendation was to the effect that six months notice be given to terminate the tenancy af the present offices in Trinity Chambers in January next. The new premises are to he handed over on December Ist. Alderman Ben- 'nett thought that whilst the new offices might be suitable temporarily, he did not think that for a permanency they were in keeping with the dignity and importance of a committee such as the Insurance Committee. He realised the need for economy at the present time, but he did not think it was wise to bind themselves for seven years. The Mayor said he had looked lon round the town, and the offices were the most suitable at the price.

NAVAL BATTLE.

BLINDED HEROES.

THREE BATTLE CRUISERS & £l2O Realised At Hippodrome THREE CRUISERS LOST. Matinee.

Five Destroyers Lost And Six

Missing.

ENEMY LOSSES "SERIOUS." The Press Bureau issued the following com- launication last night • • On the afternoon of Wednesday, 31st May, a naval engagement took place off' the coast of Jutland. • The British ships on which the brunt of the fighting fell were the Battle Cruiser Fleet an•i some cruisers and light cruisers, supported by . four battleships. . The German Battle Fleet, aided -by low visi- bility' avoided prolonged action with our main , forces, and soon after these appeared on tho scene the enemy returned to port, though not before receiving severe damage from our Vattle- ' ships. , The battle cruisers Queen Mary, Indefatigable - and Invincible; and the 'cruisers Defence and Black Prince were sunk. The Warrior was dis- abled, and after being towed for some time had to be abandoned by her crew. It is also known that the destroyers Tip- perary, Turbulent, Fortune, Sparrow Hawk, and Ardent were lost and six others are not yet accounted for. No British battleship or light cruisers were Bunk.'

===== 0002601_19160603_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
CERIVIAN LOSSES.

The enemy losses were serious. At least one battle cruiser was destroyed and lone severely ,damaged. One battleship is re- ported sunk by our destroyers during a night attack. Two light 'cruisers were disabled and probably sunk. The exact number .of enemy destroyers disposed of during the action cannot, be ascertained with any certainty, but it must have been large.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
ENEMY REPORT.

Heavy Engagement " Favour- able To Germans."

A German official message states:— . A series of heavy engagements developed bl- tween Skagerack and the Horse Reef, which took a favourable course to the Germans. Germans destroyed ,as far as known, the battle- ship Warspite, battle cruisers Queen Mary and Indefatigable ;.two armoured Cruisers, probably of the Achilles class, one small cruiser, and ships Turbulent, Nestor and Alcaser, a great number of torpedo boat destroyers, one sub- marine. A number of big battleships were damaged. Battleship Marlborough was struck by torpedo. Only two survivors of the ' Indefatigable ha' o been rescued. • The Germans admit the loss of small cruisers Wiesbaden, Pommern, Frauenlof and some torpedo boats.

SOLDIERS ALL.

Derby Recruits Who Are Enjoying Life.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
"SUMMER TIME" IN CAMP.

By PRIVATE W. J. LEAPEII.

Private W. J. Leaper is one of four members of " The Examiner" Editorial Staff who have g°ne forth to fight the pillagers of Belgium and the destroyers of the Lusitania.

"Any complaints?" the Orderly Officer de- Manded, as he opened the door of our hnt. " No, sir, but the Orderly has gone for them," replied one of the hungry-looking men who sat at the table waiting very impatiently for their mid-day meal. "Gone for what F" ejaculated the officer in surprise. - - .

"-For the plates, sir," was the answer, and all the hut laughed at the mistake of their comrade. The officer smiled as he turned to leave the hut.

It is marvellously surprising how one can mis- hear the spoken word. Of course, the "Tommy" who replied, to the officer had r mistaken " plaints" for "plates," and consequently had made himself look a fool. But the worst case Of this kind that has come to my. knowledge occurred on the first day of my Army life. T Was one of twenty recruits who had come up in Group 6 under Lord Derby's scheme. We had been before the medical officer, and he had sorted." us for either home defence or foreign service, and now we were before the Colonel to be distributed amongst the various com- Panics. The method of distribution was accord- ing to trade or profession. Being a journalist, I.was put in the company of a Tot of bank Clerks and school teachers. We were termed the " University " Company, though, as a 'natter of fact, not one, in ten of the company bad ever seen the inside of the University.

The Colonel had asked about a dozen of us What was our business in civil life, and turning to the next man, he asked. "What were you before you joined the Army?"

"Birkenhead," was the astounding reply. "What did you do? What was your work ?- almost shouted the Colonel. "It's in Cheshire, near Liverpool," replied till laoldier faintly. The Colonel went back to his desk and looked at the medical history sheet of the man, whos3 name was S . Ile found that S was deaf. and delegated him to a home service conipany.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ARMY OF HUMORISTS.

The Army seems to be made up of comedians. A Birkenhead youth declares that our battalion is not part of the British Army; he says it is part of Rarno's Army of comedians. Of course, some soldiers are consciously humorous, and some are unconsciously humorous. One .of the recruits 'who came up with me was asked what he would like to be when he went before the Recruiting Officer at Chester Castle. The officer wanted to know if the would-be soldier would like to be a cavalryman or if he would prefer some other branch of the Army that was- open to take recruits. His amusement as well as his amaze- fluent when he received the answer "I would like to be a Sergeant-Major" can -well be Imagined!

It is surprising how few of our soldiers take the Army seriously. Most of them seem to look 11POn their present life as a sort of great holiday that would not have been missed for worlds. Sometimes they grumble and grouse according to.the best Army traditions, but very soon they "become their normal, cheerful selves, ready to laugh and joke about any trivial incident.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
A "SUMMER-TIME" JOKE.

The latest joke is "summer-time." Because sbnamer-time is authorised by Act of Parlia- rnent it is by no means sacred, and last Sunday korning " Tommy " was provided With another Subject to grouse about, and to joke about. Reveille is 5.30 a.m., but now, for everything except in name it IS 4.30.. Now 4.30 on Sunday morning is a bit too early for a British soldier to get out of bed—at ,411 Y rate, that seems to be the general opinion 411 our camp. Accordingly, " summer-time " had t° go by the board for that morning, and though everybody was out of bed by 5.30, the new time 'registered 6.30. Confusion soon came, how- ever. We were due at Church by 9.30 (official time), but I am afraid we hai not finished breakfast by the time the service had started. So church parade was missed for once, Etnd the men who complained about " summer- tine " at 5.30 were singing its praises by 9.30. OWever,. everything is now settled, and , summer-time " is generally looked upon as a blessing. If it means nothing else, it means that We finish at 3.30 in the afternoon instead of the old 4.30. 20 life goes on in the Army. Sometimes we "toeinber there is -a, war on, but when we do, a we dismiss the thought and go' about our busi- rss .0f traireing, in the same spirit that a num- ner of trippers go to the:seaside. Mai' 24, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
SPLENDID SUCCESS.

La Somna may have been able to tell to a unit the number of people at the Blinded Heroes' Matinee at the Palace and Hippodrome on Thursday afternoon, but she not having given the figure it must be left for an observer without "fields of vision" to remark in general terpis that it was the largest crowd ever packed within the hall. Indeed, the accommo- dation was not nearly large enough for the number who wanted to enter, and an overflow concert would not have been a bad idea.

Every part .of the hall was thronged and the atmosphere became -so close that there • was a great run on the pretty Japanese fans enter- prising ladies had on sale. Bunches of charm- ing flowers were bought profusely, .and at afternoon tea-time—four o'clock—lemonade and ices came with refreshing taste. Of course, they had to be paid for, but all the time the Blinded Heroes' Fund was benefiting. The programme submitted lasted about three hours. All this week's Hippodrome artistes went through their turns for nothing but charity—and applause—and others in the pro- fession also came from Widnes and St. Helens and gladly gave their services, as well as Miss Marie Houghton, who sang a classical song. The band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment displayed its splendid capacity in Darewskrs "Shell Out" and Wallace's " Maritana," and when they dismissed for others to come vn to the stage the men in khaki stood in the wings like revue girls waiting for the next call. The proceedings throughout, were interesting and; in an interval, the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) came before the footlights to express in words the satisfaction of the audience who had over and over again emphasised their pleasure with their hands. He apologised to those who had bought tickets and been unable to gel- into the hall, hoping they would find con- solation in the knowledge that by purchasing tickets they had helped a good cause. He was proud of the fact that Warrington people had rallied to the aid of the blinded heroes and rejoiced that enjoyment had abounded over the various performances at the matinee. To the proprietors and Mr. Jackson, manager, of the Palace and Hippodrome, to the artistes, to the

Working Men"; -Committee, and to the Ladies' Committee, with Mrs. Henry Greenall at their head, he expressed his thanks for what they had done. (Applause.) It is estimated that £l2O will be realised.

THE DISAPPOINTED TICKET HOLDERS. To the Editor of " The Examiner." Sir,—Blinded Heroes Matinee. Owing to the enormous demand for tickets, I regret that a number of patrons were unable to gain admission. I feel sure, that in most cases, those who were disappointed felt that their money had been expended in the most worthy of all causes. At the same time, the committee and myself consider that in fairness to their supporters it is only right that ticket holders desiring their money refunded, may have it returned to them upon presenting their ticket at the booking office of the Hippodrome any day next week lrl- tween the hours of 11 a.m. and 2 p.m., or dur- ing the evening performance. Yours faithfully, W. A. JACKSON, Manager.

WALKING DAY.

1 Crime Under The Defence Of The

Realm Act.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—l think it is necessary in the public in- terest and for the maintenance of Warrington's honour in the part her people have played and suffered in this tragic war to protest against the decision of the organisers to have a Walk- ing Day, necessitating as it will do the cessation of labour op munitions which at this moment is vital to the Empire's safety. Think of our sons, brothers and husbands in the death grip suffering unparalleled hardships. Yet brave, true, and with undying courage ask only "Send us guns," "shells," and every kind of munition —to save our liberties and freedom, and hand down unsullied to our children a Charter sealed with blood, to which generatiens yet unborn will do homage. Think, think, think of these ,things! What a spectacle! Forges and works stopped! No throbbing engines nor belching furnaces! Who shall bring the iron crosses from Germany to the instigators of this process of starving the Army and the Navy? I was assured by a supporter of the stoppage that "It's good for trade." What a lot of ribbons will be sold; new boots also—while the Govern- ment is struggling to preserve enough leather for their fighting forces. Then millinery—this golden harvest for the milliners—Walking Day is great for this display. Can we beat Germany with feathers and- flowers and fantastic head- gear? Rather I would see a procession of • citizens bearing an emblem of mourning for the brave dead who are the gods that are saving us. Mr. Lloyd George says "The Easter holi- day; had a disastrous effect in the production of munitions," and. appeals for only one day at Whitsuntide. Several important Labour Leaders have commended very strongly this appeal to the unions, and yet in Warrington we have men arranging a display which is accom- panied by a general stoppage. "Oh, 'ye gods!" A person who induces, or incites, or assists another in suspending or. holding back the pro- duction of munitions is guilty of an offence under the Defence of the Realm Act, and there. fore trade accruing thereby. is "trading with the enemy." It is not yet too late for this matter to be reconsidered and the fair name of Warrington saved—and the religious denomina- tions have yet the chance of putting first things first—the nation's peril first. Win tho war! "All other things shall be added unto you." Apologising for this lengthy trespass.—Yours; &c, LUX.

Joe Durham.

Lance-Corporal Caldwell, who was 19 years of age, was formerly employed in the office of Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, after serving as a clerk for about twelve months in the London and North-Western Railway offices. He was educated at Heathside School, during which time he won the Walker Challenge Shield for swimming. He enlisted thirteen months ago.

.

King's Own Scottish Borderers, Mr. H. Beddall, who is also a Warrington man, was received by Mrs. Stringer, Bostock-street, Corporal Riley's sister, saying:—

"Arthur was killed the first night we came into the trenches—Thursday, the 11th. I saw him brought in. He was taken to a little ceine- tory and buried the next night. I know it will be a very hard blow for you all, for he was a nice young chap. He and I got on very well together. It will perhaps be a bit of a relief to you to know that he did not suffer a minute's pain; he was dead instantly. Break it as gently as pos- sible to his dear mother. It is very hard to have to tell you such \bad news, but you will be better off to know for certain and so save all suspense. With sincere wishes that your terrible blow will be got over by the help of God." Youngest of Four Soldier Sons.

Lance-Corporal Riley, who was 27 years of age, was single, but was engaged to be married. He enlisted in September, 1914, having served the old Volunteers for four years. In July last year he went to the Front, and wa)3 wounde& by a shell, and was in hospital at Glasgow for a time.

Three of his brothers have served, or aro serv- ing; the King. Alfred, who lost an eye in India, but subsequently served in the South African War, enlisted for the present war, but has been discharged. William is with the Rifle Brigade in the East, and Joseph, who joined the Rifle Brigade, is on munition work in Warrington. Our dead hero was the youngest of this patriotic family.

WOUNDED.

PRIVATE B. OLDROYD.

Sniped In The Back Whilst Sapping.

Private B. Oldroyd, a Highlander, whose home address is 86, Wellington-street, has been wounded. Writing to his sister, Miss M. Thompson, 50, Leonard-street, under date May 20th, he thus describes his exciting experiences and narrow escapes Last Sunday the Ger- mans bombarded us terribly with artillery and trench mortars for two and. a half hours, and I cannot explain liow I came through it, for we suffered very heavily. I was hit once or twice with bursting shrapnel, but was not wounded. Well, things went rather quietly until Monday, when the enemy blew up part of our front line with a mine. On Tuesday night at 12 o'clock I was working out at the front cutting a sap, and the bombs were bursting all around us. I had just worked my way through with the pick, and had turned round to pick up a shovel when

PRIVATE B. OLDROYD. Wounded.

I felt a stabbing pain at my back, and I knew a sniper had got me. I thought it was all up with me, for it stunned me for a few minutes.

Saved By Strap Buckle.

"An officer crawled over to see where I was hit, and he remarked, 'Lucky• chap! good job you were bending instead of standing straight up.' The bullet caught the buckle of my straps, and turned it, and then made a groove up my back, but I shall soon be all right." Private Oldroyd is a married man with three childien. He ,is 30 years of age, and, before enlisting soon after the outbreak of war, was, employed at the Mersey-street Tannery. Whilst at the Front he has had many narrow escapes. He was educated at the National School under the headmastership of the late Mr. Dewhurst.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
M. HAROLD SMITH, M.P.,

CONSCIENTIOUS 'OBJECTORS.

Mr. H. Smith, speaking in the House of Com- mons on Tuesday in the debate on the treatment of conscientious objectors, said that whilst dis.- avowing .sympathy with conscientious objectors as such, he did not believe they were receiving the treatment which Parliament, rightly or wrongly, decided they' should have. It was true that the Tribunals had had peculiar diffi- culties placed in their way, and that bodies of men who were not really conscientious objectors, but opposed to the war, had devoted themselvos to drilling these men as to the attitude they should adopt. After all, the Tribunals 'ere there to exercise a judicial mind. It would surprise many members to learn that although the rules gave in applicant the absolute right to be repre- sented by a lawyer, or by a friend, or relative, the City of London Tribunal, for instance, would not allow the applicant to ask a single question. He might be a nervous man, and quite unaccustomed to dealing with bodies of that character. Many of the questions. put by the Tribunals, moreover, were not really ques- tions of a judicial character, but questions &- signed to trip up the applicant. He was not speaking of all the Tritunals, but of a certain number. _

It was hardly possible to deny that a man who had stood buffeting and abuse and ignomy, and finally imprisonment, was really a con- scientious objector, and each one of these cases was, to his mind, a standing example, he would not say of the inefficiency of the Tribunals, but of a mistake made by them. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19160603_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
The Part Played by Tanners.

At the beginning of the war we found it impossible to Provide adequate footwear and leather equipment for our then limited Army, and had to obtain American help;, to-day we not only are completely providing for an Army of 4,000,000 to 5.000.000, but have also under- taken enormous contracts for our Allies. At the same time the wants of our civilian popu- lation have been met, and some day the story of bow all this was achieved will be inspiring, reading for a future generation of leather men. —"Leather Trades iteview.!', . • • • .

PRIVATE S. LYTHGOE. Wounded.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE T. J. WILLIAMS.

flit With Shell Fragments, But Progressing Favourably.

News has reached Mr. and Mrs. Williams, 43, Longford-street, to the effect that their son, Private Thomas Jolley Williams, of the South Lancashire Regiment, has been wounded in action.

Sergeant W. Switzer wrote, under date May 25th, as follows : _

"I have the painful duty of informing you that your son, Private T. J. Williams, was severely wounded last night, the 24th inst. I moved him to the Field Dressing Station at once, but I have heard since that he died of his wounds. I don't know whether that is true, however, for I have had no official noti- fication. He got hit with fragments of a •shell, and was unconscious from then to the time of my leaving him. During the short time he has been with us I have found him a brave, fearless and good soldier, and I don't wish to have a better one under my charge. No one

will miss him more than I. I send the deepest sympathy of myself and platoon." " P.S.-official news has just come to hand, that your son has died of his wounds." This P.S., however, appears to be incorrect, for Private Williams has written the following letter, under date May 27th—three days later than Sergt. Switzer's letter : " I expect you will have heard by now that] got knocked about a bit. I got wounded by shrapnel in the left arm, thigh, and a few splinters in both legs, but I am going on fine now, and the shrapnel was taken out last Wednesday. I might get to 'Blighty' when 1 am a bit better." Private Williams, who is 21 years of age, enlisted on the Ist September, 1914, having been previously employed as a moulder by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., where he had worked since leaving school. He was drafted to the Dardanelles in June, and in November was sent home with frost-bitten feet. He was an inmate of the Netley Hos- pital for a month, and, after recovering, was sent to the theatre of war, in which he has been wounded. He was educated at St. Ann's School. His father, who is a wiredrawer by trade, but who .has had to relinquish that occupation through ill-health, enlisted the same week as his son, but was discharged after about five weeks' service. He is at present an employe of the Alliance Box Company, Ltd., Orford-lane.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR FOOD SUPPLY.

MR. RYLANDS. AND INVAWN 'Can Any More Men Be Spared DANGER. - From The Farms?

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

, Sir,—Only those whose vision penetrates no farther than the end. of their noses will differ from Mr. ItAanda on the questinn of invasirin. It may be unpardonable dullness on my part, but I fail •to•see the fairness of the Tribunal insisting, on a- tradesmen, with domestic responsibilities and limited leisure, parading at the Drill Hall, while the badged clerk (who , wields the heavy pen for some six or eight hours a day) is at liberty to. parade his precious per- sonality along the principal streets of the town during week nights, and at Pickmere during the week-ends.

Apart from the question of invasion, there are men required for fire brigade and simillr services. Why are these duties left to the fathers and grandfathers to fulfil, some of whom are themselves working on munitions? The most telling argument against thaVolunteer system far, the Regular Forces was its unfairness. The same unfairness is manifest on this question of volunteering during one's leisure. The compulsory principle may well be applied to this question.

CHACITN SELON SES FACULTIES.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—lt is sincerely tO be hoped that the appeal made in your last Saturday's issue for Volunteers will not be dismissed lightly by your readers.

Important military duties have been entrusted

to the Warrington Company, and the officer commanding has at present only a quarter of the men required to carry out the work effi- ciently. Under .the Volunteer Act men from the age of 17 and upwards, fairly physically fit, can be enrolled, men of military age being amenable to the Military Service Act. It certainly would be deplorable and a refiec-

tion upon the men left behind, after the sacri- fices made by the men already gone, if the older men and those exempt from the provi- sions of the Service Act do not offer to assist in an elementary principle of national duty. Recreation is essential and necessary in these strenuous days, but the indiments of drill can be acquired with very little sacrifice of time and leisure; two hours per week is surely no great self-denial.

- It is asserted that the dilatory methods of the War Office are to be blamed for the movement not progressing. This is only partly true. The Government is influenced by public opinion and demand, and the only instrument to supply the motive power to place the Volunteer mpvement upon a tangible and definite basis is the quick- ening, or rather the development, of the latent national spirit upon organised and directed lines, and it is up to the men remaining to do it.

A very gratifying feature to learn is that the recommendations of the local Tribunal are now bearing fruit. Exempted men are now being put through their recruits' course at a fairly rapid rate. This factor alone justifies the con- tinuance of, and support to, the movement. Applicants desiring to enrol can do so any Monday or Thursday night at 8 o'clock, at the Drill Hall. 4, Bridge Foot, June 2nd.

W. ASHTON.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
VOLUNTEERS FOR FARM WORK.

Suggestion By Mr. F. Aylmer Frost.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir--A question causing the farmers con- siderable anxiety is, "How are we to get in the harvest this year?" Those I have spoken to on. the subject think

that the usual number of Irishmen. will not come over, on account of the troubles in their country, the danger of enemy submarines, and the fear of being drafted into the Army if once on this side of the water.

I suggest to overcome the difficulty, to some extent at any rate, that the Volunteers (V.T.C.) should offer their services to the farmers in their respective neighbourhoods fOr a certain number of hours per week, and that the farmers should pay to the various Corps the- amount it would cost in ordinary times to do the work performed by the Volunteers. 4 • This scheme would act to the nation's advan- tage in many ways. It would find a large por- tion of the labour necessary to garner the har- vest quickly, before it had time to be spoilt by lying out too long. It would provide funds towards the cost of equipment and necessary outlay, which the Government does not provide for the Volunteers. It would exercise the muscles of the men, which may be required later on (or at any time) for trench digging. It would tend to create a feeling of satisfac- tion to the Volunteers themselves by knowing that they were doing some tangible good to the nation, in addition to providing elementary training for men about to join the Regular Forces—doing certain guard duty, and prepar- ing themselves for national defence, if required. Many Volunteers lead sedentary lives in our towns, and this would be an inducement for them to spend more hours in the country, to the benefit of their health. In the circumstances I should think few

people, if any, would object to this work being carried on on Sundays within reasonable hours, as well as on week days. There has been a general thinning out process going on for some time in all Volunteer Corps. Those whose patriotism has been too severely tried by lack -of suitable recognition from the Government having withdrawn from the move- ment, hence the stalwarts left may be counted upon to do anything in their power to help the Empire, which is so dear to them, in any way they can. The hay harvest is almost upon us. Cannot something be done in the-way suggested, before it is "just too. late"? Yours-faithfully, F. A. FROST, Platoon Commander, Cheshire Volunteer Regiment. Grappenhall Hall, June 2nd, 1916.

A SUGGESTION TO THE MILITARY.

I The: Chairman of the Warrington Rural Tribunal (Mr. G. Mather), at the sitting on Wednesday, referred to the further 'calls to be made on men engagetio in agricul- ture and the effect upon the food supply. He said they knew very well that the Work on the farms was about a month behind the usual time. He had Written to the military author- ities making a suggestion that something ought to be arranged whereby -the men of military age engaged on .farms could bontinue their work in order to grow as much food as possible for the nation; .and .at the .same time,,be enrolled • for some .kind of military duties-4h the lilies, of the Volunteer Training Corps. Colonel Mocatta, in' xleply, .commended tilt., idea and said if it were carried out it would prove all right, but unfortunately they had no drill instructors.' Later, he (Mr. Mather) communi- cated with the, Board of Agriculture in the matter, and he had received a reply that it ,Ns ould receive their immediate attention. He had also had a letter from Mr. W. Peter Itylands, the commandant of the Warrington Volunteer Training Corps, suggesting certain procedure. He felt very strongly on the mat- ter, as it would be a great mistake if they took any more men from tile farms. Mr. Joshua Ball said he agreed with the remarks of the Chairman about the serious condition of agriculture, but unless there was some power more than the voluntary organisa- tion behind the movement to train the men he was afraid it would not be a success.

Mixed Farming and Drilling.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell thought they were asking too much from the men in expecting them to drill after a hard day's work on a farm.

The Chairman: There is 130 comparison be- tween the position of the man who stayed at home, however hard his lot, and the soldier who has gone to the trenches. Mr. W. Furness (the military representative) pointed out that other Tribunals were advising men who were exempted to join Volunteer Trainino. Corps. Several members interposed with the remark I that such movements had met with little suc- cess in surrounding districts. Mr. Furness (continuing) said the Govern- ment did not seem prepared either to approve or sanction the volunteer movement. Instead of "encouraging it they were throwing cold water on the scheme.

The Chairman: It is no use discussing the proposal if we are not unanimous. I have some feeling in the matter. I think the military authorities ought to be informed—Lord Kitch- ener, or whoever it is who is responsible. A Member: How would you like Lord Kitchener to come here and teach you how to farm?

St. Paul's Precedent.

A claim for exemption was made by a brick- layer, of Croft, on the ground that he was an Independent Methodist parson. -Re stated that he followed a secular occupation to maintain himself. The churches appointed their own' ministers, and all duties of a minister were carried out by them, such as preaching, pas- toral, baptisms, presiding at the Lord's Supper. Fifty young men had enlisted from the dis- trict, and in consequence many sad homes needed their care and sympathy. Many of \their ministers were aged and could not travel. 4. circular enclosed along with the application intimated that the War Office recognised the ministers of the denomination as coming with- in the term for exemption from military ser- vice.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said the applicant, like SC Paul, who was a tent-maker, followed an occupation which was to his credit. He wished they could all minister for nothing. It would be very nice indeed. From his occupa- tion he knew how to build up a spiritual house as well as a material one. . Conditional exemption was granted.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
An Afflicted Home.

In a domestic claim for absolute exemption a married man, a joiner, living at Glazebrook, said his wife had been an invalid for the past two years and had been bedfast since Christ- mas. If he had to go the shock would, in his opinion, kill her. He also had an invalid sister-in-law living with him. With food so dear and the sickness he was quite unable to pay for help. The appeal was adjourned for a fortnight for a medical certificate as to his wife's health.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
Fearnhead Nurseryman's Big Business.

A nurseryman and market gardener, residing at Fearnhead, married, in an appeal for exemp- tion, stated that he had four shops and two market stalls in Warrington and Earlestown. His output of tomatoes was about eight tons each year. All his fruit and vegetables were, as far as possible, sold in his own shops. He had only one experienced man besides himself left to do the work. He had a motor lutTy and when going to Liverpool he carted Govern- ment castings for a Warrington firm. If `.he left the business would have to close down. He had never taken a holiday. At the last sitting of the Tribunal he appealed for the exemption of his brother, who worked with him in the business.

The Tribunal adjourned the hearing until August 17th, when the two applications will be decided.

TANNERS' CLAIMS.

The Conflicting Needs Of The Yards And The Army.

SINGLE BEFORE MARRIED.

At the Rural Tribunal on Wednesday, The Penketh Tannery Co., Ltd., applied for the exemption of nine of their employes. A representative of the firm stated that the work on which the men were engaged could not be done by women or old men. The men were already working overtime, and very hard at that too, in some cases 13 „.hours per day. No more pressure could be brought to bear upon them, and if the staff were further depleted the output would be still further reduced. After soine discussion the Chairman (Mr. G. Mather) announced that all the cases would be adjourned for 21 days to see what could be done. The Tribunal were of the opinion that the firm had better see the military authorities to ascer- tain what was their attitude.

Earlier in the proceedings a member of the Tribunal (Mr. Stevenson) expressed the opinion that the Tribunal ought to try and get a num- ber of the single men out of the works before they began to deal with the applications of married men.

Ex-Soldier as Handyman at Padgate. Out of eight candidates, three of whom were personally interviewed, Mr. Thomas Champion, of 158, Padgate-lane, was, on Wednesday, ap- pointed by the Board of Guardians to the position of handyman at Padgate Cottage Homes In his autobiographical details Mr. Champion mentioned that he served in the South African War with the Royal Engineers. They gathered a large number of hunches of wild flowers, and expressed the ' &sire to have these rambles periodically during the summer.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR ATTITUDE TOWARDS

GERMANY.

am pleased to find myself for once in absolute agreement with lir. O'Brien. We have certainly a noble theme to deal with, worthy of abler pens than ours. namely, "the resettle- ment of Europe after the war." 1 Germany having started .the military concert and failed to carry through its objective, must .naturally pay the cost both in hard cash and territory. As our friend -says, /Germany- or Prussia, has set her own precedents, and on those lines she must be treated. In her past wars the sword of the Hohenzollerns has always exacted its full pound of flesh; it has not only diminished its victim's acres, but has also drained his purse, so those who sow the wind must reap the whirlwind. Taking the period of history at which I anti Mr. O'Brien seem mutually to, agree upon—the Danish War of 1864—1 maintain that all terri- tory acquired by the two Central Powers from that date ought to be restored to its original owners.. Schleswig-Holstein with Heligoland and the control of the Kiel Canal to Denmark; there should be substantial guarantees in money and territory to Belgium; France should have her lost provinces of Alsace-Lorraine restored; Italy should have the really natural defensive frontier that should include all Italian speak- ing people; Austria should give up the Slav provinces of Bosnia and Herzegovina, and the Sanjak of Novibazar, and these should be in- corporated in the late Kingdom of Serbia to form a Greater Serbia with an ample coast- line on the Adriatic; Montenegro ought to be also suitably rewarded by territory with a coast- line at the expense of Turkey. The Kingdoms of Hanover, Bohemia and. Poland ought, if they so desired, to be placed in a state of indepen- dence as distinct from subject States. In the resettlement of the world at the close of the war steps should be taken to neutralise the various narrow water passages such as the Kiel Canal and the entrance to the Baltic, the Straits of Gibraltar, the Turkish traits, the Suez Canal and the Panama Canal.

In regard to finance, I am not so well up :n the matter, and leave it for better brains than mine to solve, simply quoting the estimate of Mr. E. Crammond, of the Liverpool Stock Ex- change, who calculates that the cost of the war to the Allies up to March 31st, 1917, will be as follows:

• Belgium France

Great Britain (net) ..

2,600,000,000

Italy Russia

500,000,000 2,400,000,000

Serbia and Montenegro Total

£8,600,000,000

Mr. Crammond further points out that "be- fore thewar Germany pothessed over 5,000,000 tons of merchant shipping, or about 10 per cent. of the total merchant shipping of the world; and, it was her deliberate aim to destroy as much merchant shipping as possible,. so that when peace came her proportion of the total might-be much greater." He suggests that "the German merchant fleet should be applied' first in making good the loss which each maritime power, belligerent or neutral, had suffered 'through German submarines and mines. Germany should be compelled to return in kind, not in money, the rolling stock and machinery, raw material, manufactured goods, works of art, &c., which she had stolen from Belgium, Northern France, Poland and Serbia." After all this has been done he calculates that there will still remain in 'favour of the Allies a-balance of £8,000,000,000 still to be liquidated. These appear to be enormous demands, but they are small in comparison to what German avarice would demand if she were victorious. All these items take no account of the lives of our valiant and heroic soldiers, lost in this world-wide struggle for simple right to live in peace and amity with all men. Nothing will compensate us for the loss of those noble hearts who have fallen in. the strife, and whose golden deeds of heroism in the great war will be told in the future ages to our children and our chil- dren's children.

There is still one more result to be referred to as being brought about by the present conflict. It has brought about the fact that there must be considerable changes inside the BritiA Empire, and steps will have to be taken to broaden the basis of the British Commonwealth by federating the various parts. By creating a Federal Government and Parliament of all the Dominions along with the United Kingdom, and that steps must be taken to bring up to a higher -plane the civilisation of the subject races.

In conclusion, let me say there must he some- thing noble in a cause that unites in the struggle for freedom such diverse countr;es as Constitu- tional Britain, Repuhlican Prance, and Despotic Russia. There is something inspiring when you see Protestant, Roman Catholic and Orthodox Christians standing shoulder to shoulder in one common cause

PhIL S (.4 OD.

Away From the Danger Zone. Most people know that all leave from the Front was stopped just before Easter and have hitherto assumed that the stoppage was due to the exigencies of the situation. To Mr. Peto, an earnest seeker after truth, as befits his name, Mr. Tennant admitted, that there was another reason. Last year, it seems, some returning warriors got so mixed up in the con- gested Easter traffic that they never reached home at all, so this year the authorities re- solved to keep them out of the danger zone."— " Punch."

CAFE CHANTANT.

Lady Harmood Banner Commends Home For Waifs And Strays.

One of the pleasantest events held in Stockton Heath in recent months was that of a cafe chantant and sale in aid of a Warrington insti- tution, on Tuesday afternoon and evening. Fashionably dressed ladies flocked to the open- ing ceremony in the Church Institute, and there heard the claims of the St. George's Home for Waifs and Strays put vividly before them. As a pleasant means of raising money for charit- able objects, the cafe chantant has come to stay. The institute was beautifully decorate.", bunting being strung from corner to corner crosswise. At the back of the gorgeously flower- bedecked platform were bunched several Union Jacks. Two or three scores of whist tables looked attractive under the afternoon-tea fancy cloths, and surrounding this array stood a num- ber of stalls tastefully covered with ornamental and useful articles, flowers, or refreshments.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
Silver Thimble League Record.

Before introducing Lady Harmood Banner (wife of the Chairman of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' Coal and Iron Co.', Ltd)., Mr. Brad- stock Lockett, diocesan treasurer, explained that the Silver Thimble League, by whom th?. affair had been organised, was really a wonder- ful organisation, for since 1898 in various parts of the country it had raised from fourteen to fifteen thousand pounds. If it had not been for the sales they promoted, their homes would not have prospered as they had done. They had a beautiful little home in Salisbury-street, War- rington, and many past inmates were rendering valiant services for the country. One of the Nye was on Admiral Beatty's flagship, the Lion. Ninety-nine out of a hundred who went into the home "made good" when they came out of it. A feature was that the boys did not have to wear uniform and thus be branded as objects of pity, but wore ordinary clothing kindly sent by neighbours. -Such wearing apparel was pre- ferred by the children, and there was hlso a saving in their upkeep. Over 1,000 boys trained in their homes were serving in the Army an.' Navy, and now resident were 600 motherless children, whose fathers were on active service. Lady Banner Opens Sale.

Lady Harmood Banner said that with such facts before them they could not do otherwise than give of their best. The children must be looked after and protected. She had great pleasure in declaring the sale open, for it was, indeed, a worthy cause. (Applause.) Canon Willis (rector of Warrington) proposed a vote of thanks to Lady Banner for opening the sale and observed that from these institu- tions some splendid specimens had been turned out.

_ Major the Rev. W. Bracecamp, chaplain to His Majesty's Forces, who happened to be spending a few hours in the district, was pre- sent at the ceremony, and seconded the vote of thanks. In their homes, he said, they were try- ing to provide a good substitute for the mothers of the boys. The sale began, accompanied by some Scotch songs by Privates J. Owen and C. Todd, who had come from Thelwall Heys Convalescent Home to display their tunefulness, vivacity, and kilts. Throughout the afternoon and evening vocal solos and duets, as well as pianoforte selections, were given in acceptable style. The proceeds amounted to £125.

LYMM'S FLAG DAYS.

Over £lOO For Lord Roberts Memorial Fund.

Last week-end a great effort was made in this district on,behalf of the Lord Roberts Memorial Fund, which provides workshops for our maimed soldiers and sailors. Various methods of collecting money were used, including a house-to-house collection, collecting boxes placed at various establishments, and the sale of flags in the streets. The district was divided up into seven wards, and each ward collected independently and was in charge of a president. The exact 'result of 'collections made in each ward was as follows: Lymm Ward, £25 Os. 7d.; Heatley; £2O us. 3d.; Booth's Hill, £l4 13s. 2d.; statham, £l2 19s. 3d.; Warburton, £lO 19s. 2d.; Partington, •£9 7s. 10d.; Thelwall, £3 is.; Drawing-room Meeting, £9 ss. The Rev. G. B Thurston was chairman, and Mr. F. E. Evans hon. secretary. After the expenses had been deducted a cheqpe • for £lO2 14s. 7d. was for- warded to Mrs. Patton, of Wythenshawe, the president of the district.

THE EXAMINNR, SATURDAY, JUNE 3, 1916.

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC. The flag-selling for the Lord Roberts Memorial Fund last week-end created quite a sensation.

On stepping out of your back garden you were instantly besieged by at least a score of fair maidens, all tendering•their wares.

Nor did they ask you to buy. They just pinned one in your coat—then handed you the box.

Perhaps the keenest of them all were to be found in the neighbourhood of the station.

Not even the engine driver or the stoker were allowed to pass by unchallenged.

One lady was actually seen nursing another lady's baby while she bought a flag.

An interesting cricket match is shortly to take place—Grammar School Boys v. Wounded Soldiers from Brookfield.

The running part of the game seems to be the only objection the wounded soldiers have to raise.

Several local soldiers are expected to be home from the Front this week-end.

Amongst them, I believe, will be Corporal Henry Brookes, the first local soldier to receive a war decoration.

He was awarded. the Order of St. George (2nd Class) for heroic ambulance work under fire.

When is this gallant soldier to be publicly congratulated for the honour that he has be- stowed upon the village?

There is no time like the present!

The services at the Congregational Church next Sunday will be conducted by a blind minister.

During the hearing of the Council appeals at the Tribunal last Friday night all Council members were asked to retire.

In Case of Smallpox.—The Council, at their monthly meeting, on Monday, authorised Dr. Burrows, the deputy medical officer, to make arrangements, if need be, for an isolation hospital for smallpox patients.

Fire Brigade Appointments.—Mr. William Rose has been appointed captain of the Fire Brigade at a Salary of £lO ss. per annum, and Mr. Joseph Leah, lieutenant, at £3 158. Coun- cillor Ingham told the Council on Monday that on the previous Thursday the horses went for a run of several miles and, in case .of fire, he was sure they would he equal to the task. Does Not Know Whether He Is A Soldier Or A Sailor.—Mrs." Mantle, of Sandy-lane, has re- ceived word from her son, Driver A. Mantle, of the South Lancashire Regiment, to the effect that he is well and hearty. During his Army career he has been in the East, and is now serv- ing with the Indian Expeditionary. Force as a transport driver. He has been so many times (In the water, he says, that he does not know whether he is a soldier or a sailor. Driver Mantle is 22 years of age, and enlisted at the outbreak of war, being drafted out on active service after a few months' training down South. Prior to the war he was employed by his father as a carter.

True Dog Story.—A correspondent writes:— "How is it that some dogs are so antagonistic to officials in uniform? They -appear to engender a marked dislike to postmen and police- men in particular. One such species of the canine tribe was brought to my notice this week. It had severely bitten the village post- man whilst delivering at the residence of a worthy magistrate. The postman duly notified the owner, who had the little brute brought before him. Gravely peering down at the little terrier, the worthy legal gentleman asked if he was penitent, but a savage snarl was the only response. It was decided to inflict the extreme penalty of the law, and the unhappy little dog was led away to a place of execution, where the sentence was duly carried out amidst great sorrow, particularly among the lady members of the family.

THE EXTRA Is. Id.

Council Faced With CircumstancPs Over Which They Have No Control.

ALL GOT THEIR BIT AT STAKE.

An increase of is. id. in the £ on the rates, making 4s. 4d. in all, requires some explanation and justification, and that was given at the monthly meeting of the Urban Council on Mon- day evening, when the new figure was adopted. The Chairman (Coun. F. Lockyer) said that owing to illness he was not at the General Purposes Committee when the new rate was fixed, but he came to the conclusion that the decision they arrived at would be for the bene- fit of the district they represented. If there were any who would feel the change more than others it was the tradespeople; and they were all included in that. ' Councillor Cowper: I'm not a tradesman. Councillor Critchley : What are you? Councillor Cowper: An employer of labour. Continuing, the Chairman said they all had their bit at stake—some more than others—and all they would do was to submit to it. Councillor Ingham pointed out that the 4s. 4d. was made up of a general district rate of 3s. 9d. for ordinary purposes and a special hospital rate of 7d. in the £ for one year. It should be made quite clear that the whole of the is. id. extra was not intended to be a permanent rate. Comments of the Villagers. •

Remarking that a good deal of comment had been made in the village regarding the increase, Councillor Gotobed said that in fixing the new rate they were up against three chief factors. One was the large number of empty houses at a high rateable value. Unfortunately, the largest houses in the district were vacant. The second factor was the very serious epidemic of scarlet fever and diphtheria. That, of course, was the greatest cause of all. They had had it for several months and, unfortunately, it Was not yet at an end. The third considerable item was the interest on loans. They had had to pay up the old loans at a small rate of interest and take up new loans, which, owing to the war, were at a greater rate of interest. Those were all circumstances over which the Council had no control. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Groves was of opinion that the 7d. proposed to be levied did not pay for all they would want for the hospital, but when t!aer got the hospital clear of the infectious cases there would not be much expense in its upkeep. Councillor Cowper said he was pleased to see that the Council were laying a rate sufficiently high' to enable them to pay their debts. Councillor Parker.then formally,proposed and Councillor Cowper seconded the fixing of the rates at 4s. 4d. in the 2, and this was agreed to unanimously.

PRIVATE HARRY TAYLOR.

Killed While Eating Breakfast.

News has this week been received to the effect that Private Harry Taylor, of the Cheshire Regiment, has made the supreme sacrifice. The news was obtained in the first place through the medium of a letter sent by a comrade, Private Ernest Wilson, which stated that the gallant soldier had met his death by a shell Which dropped in his dugout while he was partaking of breakfast, killing several and wounding others. This was closely followed by a letter sent by the gallant soldier's Company-Sergeant-Major, ex- pressing his sympathy with the relatives; also stating that he was killed by a shell, and that death was instant.

Private Harry Taylor was a well-known local

PRIVATE HARRY TAYLOR.

Killed.

man, and resided with his parents at Rush Green. He was 25 years of ago. He was edti- cated at Oughtrington School and worshipped at Oughtrington Church. He enlisted in the Cheshire Regiment in January, 1915, and was drafted out to France last Christmas. Prior to the war he was employed as a, hay drawer by Mr. Samuel Davies, of Platt Farm, Heatley. His brother, Private George Taylor, late of the Cheshire Regiment, has just recently been ia- valided out of the Army, suffering from a bayonet wound received while on active service a few months ago. He was endeavouring to jump across a trench when he accidentally slipped, and fell on the bayonet, which passed right through the lower part of the abdomen. After lying between life and death for several months in a base hospital, he was eventually transferred to a hospital in Lopdon, and from there received his discharge.

DRUMMER HERBERT THOMASON

%Vell-known Bandsman Wounded ,And

Suffering From Shock.

Mrs. Herbert Thomason, df Cross Brow, Statham, has this week received an official com- munication from the War Office to the effect that her husband, Drummer Herbert Thomason, of the Cheshire Regiment, is at present in hos- pital suffering, from wounds and shell shock. Drummer Thomason is a well-known local

DRUMMER H. THOMASON.

Wounded

man, and is a prominent member of the Lymm Subscription band. He is 27 years of age, and was educated at Oughtrington School. Prior to the war he worshipped at the Lymm Parish Church. He enlisted with several local lads in September, 1914, and was drafted out to France in September? 1915.

CORPORAL WILFRED SHAW.

In Hospital Undergoing Operation.

Mrs. Shaw, of Orchard-road, Sandy-lane, has this week received news to the effect that her son, Corporal Wilfred Shaw, of the A.S.C., has been admitted into hospital. Corporal Shaw has recently been over on furlough from the Front, and arrived back only to find that he had to undergo an operation to have two bows taken out of his knee. The latest news received states that he is in a base hospital undergoing the necessary preparations prior to the opera- tion. 'Corporal W. Shaw is a well-known local footballer, playing for several seasons with the Oughtrington Club. He was a prominent mem- ber,of the famous_Lymm Athletic Football Club during the season that they won the League Championship. He is 26 years of age, and enlisted in September of 1914, and was. drafted out to the Front in May, 1915. Prior to the war he was employed as a bricksetter by Mr. H. Thomason, builder, of Dog Bridge. His brother, Lance-Corporal Jack Shaw, has ben with him throughout the whole campaign, and is expected to be home on furlough very shortly.

PRIVATE FRED MOSS.

Local Plumber Reported Missing.

Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Moss, of the Chapel House, Higher-lane, have this week received news to the effect that their son, Private Fred Moss, Cheshire Regiment, is reported missing. The news was received in a letter sent by a comrade, Corporal G. Owen, which read : "I am deeply grieved at having to report that your son Fred has been reported missing

PRIVATE FRED MOSS. Missing.

since last Sunday night. Since then I have been making inquiries, and was yesterday informed that he and Joseph Antrobus were still reported missing. I went out to find where the remainder of the battalion was, and found Percy Appleton, and William and Tom Read, all comrades, an:l they told me that if was quite correct that Fred and Joseph Antrobus were missing. Then I asked if his cousin, Corporal Charles Moss had got to know, and they said that they did not think he did, and that all I could do was to in- form you at once. We are still making in- quiries about them, and as soon as we get to know anything further we will let you know immediately. We all feel the loss of them already, for wherever we went they were always such bright fellows, and always used to liven things up." Private Fred Moss was a well-known local lad, having been educated at Lymm School. He worshipped at the Baptist Church. He is 20 years of age, and was employed as a plumtor by Henry Burgess, Eagle Brow. He enlisted in September, 1914, and was drafted out to France in September, 1915. He took a deep interest in various sports, and was a prominent member of the Lymm Lads' Club. Letters received later in the week still report them missing.

PROLOGUE.

" Come along, Cieser, we must escape those devils, or we shall never see Warwickshire again!" Shouted Sir Arthur Stanleigh to his madly-straining horse as the great white hunter tore along the tortuous path of a Virginian wood. He had ridden hard after the hounds in his native county, but Ulla evening the need for speed was greater than ever before. Alas! the animal's supreme effort was in vain. Where the path became a mere track Indians in cool, mad rage were awaiting the Eng- lishman. As the horse toiled up the path, spent with the gallop, naked savages leaped npon horse and rider, and to the Indian encampment the captors bore the scion of a noble Englisk house. It had been policy for Sir Arthur Stanleigh to I eave England for a while; but he had not learnt ills lesson, and in far Virginia his gallantries to an Indian maid placed him in' dire peril. His escapade etcled in capture, and on a .beautiful ever4ag in june, 1632, the charms and delights ota Virginian sunset held no attraction for him—the tribe had condemned him to torture by fire till he died. Man- fullY he met his doom. He would show these rude, uncultured, naked savages how an Englishman Could die. Wild Sir Arthur did not whine; he accepted his fate as a gambler. Be had gambled and lost—that was all. His last card had been ttunleed. and now he was "to go out" by the most terrible form of death. Better men than he had died at the stake. That fact would not stay the Pain of the flames as in a few moments now they would scorch and sear and peel the flesh from his tones. Tied to the stake by raw thongs, he cautiously strained his bonds for a loose knot. It was useless; the Indians knew their work too well, and. dancing round him, chanting the death song cf an enemy, they watched the moon climb high. Then as the torch was thrust forward to light the staggots around the feet of the captive the whole 'leavens blazed into sudden glory. Quick-witted and alSit, by a mighty effort Sir Arthur wrenched one !,4111 free. Pointing to the falling meteor, he cried, ;My sign. The Great Spirit is displeased that you should dare to put to the torture a Great White Chief, Loose the Vonds!" .The Stanleigh of Warwickshire who dared to claim Kicellie with the supernatural commanded the Indians to set him free, and the savages, a few Minutes before loud in song as they danced the death dirge of the doomed pale-face, did as they were bid, and Sir Arthur Stanleigh became an hon- "Thred,' feared, and autocratic guest. it "n en the meteorite cooled he found imbedded in theal" nornbinienee diamond. After a week of feasting Diam ..letnen rode away, carrying with him The • ori'L from the Sky—the "Stanleigh charm ?igainst harm."

CHAPTER I. A HERITAGE or HATE.

Hy some strange chance, two hundred and fifty Years later, the Diamond from the Sky—a gem fatalistic and benevolent in turn—is again in Vir- ginia. The will of wild Sir Arthur laid it down that when the family became extinct in England the heir was to be sought among the sons of the American family of Stanleighe in Virginia. And 110‘v, in the spring of 1882, Cecil, eighth Earl of Stanleigh was nearing the end of his days in his Warwickshire castle. Peeling that he had not long to live the Earl sent Itfarrnaduke Smythe, his lawyer, to Virginia, and }a,a man of the law, greatly fearing Indians, of Whom he had read and heard much, but seen moth- ,hlng, started on his journey in fear and trembling. Iro of the family awaited his coming. A bitter existed between Colonel Arthur Stanleigh of "rginia and his cousin, Judge Lamar Stanleigh. The latter had a young son, but a child had yet to be born to the young wife of the Colonel, and he ,waa not too pleased to read a letter from his English 4insman :

"tEAa KINSILAX.—Under British law at my death tt become Earl of Stanleigh. As you have refused 'ne title, the eon of Judge Lamar. Stanleigh sue- to the title and estates unless at that time you .ave a son. The Diamond from the Sky also goes the heir.—Your affectionate kinsman,

" ST/LVLEIGH."

Au 1 English title had no charm for the Virginian `nBtocrat, but it was another matter that both title :diamond should go to the eon of his detested "Never mind," said the Colonel to his lifelong friend, Dr. Henry Lee, "the baby will be tboY, and the Judge and his family can go hang. • at little brat, hateful son of a miserable father, shall never be the Earl of Stanleigh." Judge Lamar Stanleigh had also heard from the tarl. and he awaited with impatience news from 18Xileigh Hall which would decide the question of , succession. Ruminating deeply upon Lhe mere :Tegance of the honour falling to his boy, Blair. the "Licige rode to the edge of his estate. Never an Zflible man, that afternoon he was more irritable usual, and the sight of a gipsy encampment ;481de his boundary roused the beast within him. What do I care," he shouted to Matt Harding, about your wife? Her condition is nothing to ble. Get off my land." If Judge Stanleigh doesn't want you on his land You can camp on mine," said a voice behind them;. Itlid turning round with a snarl, the Judge saw Colonel Stanleigh. Of course, you encourage any scum," snapped '4e Judge as he rode away. , Learning the need of Hagar, the Colonel handed Cr a banknote, and said to her husband: "The doctor will be at my house all the evening. Should Y°ll heed him for your wife, come for him." It was a fateful night for three families in Fair- the destinies of all were bound up in the ,alDDenings of the next few hours. Quite early Dr. !lee was called by Matt Harding, and later he said the cipsy : "You may say aa was said of old, Par Ant() us a child is born, unto us a son is The words conveyed little to the man, • tit in the tent Hagar's mother-heart was rejoicing in the possession of a new life vouchssifed to her. Marrnaduke Smythe, as a man of the law, had ;Zritten to Judge Stanleigh that he would arrive in 'zairfax almost as soon as his letter, and the Vir- 44lian. anxious to do honour to the representative

of his kinsman, sent his coloured servant to the station to see if a stranger came by the evening train.

"Yaks. suh," said the man. "Der was a strange gemmun got off the train, but he didn't say nuffin' to me."

The lawyer, full of the fear of savage Indians, approached a porter with the query, "Is there any danger of Indians?" _ _

Spotting a green 'tin, that worthy replied, "Sure. We often have to barricade ourselves in the station and use our guns." With this comfortable assurance Marmaduke was left alone, and sat on his trunk in the station yard to consider his desperate condition. Drawing his revolver, he bemoaned leaving peaceful Warwick- shire, and was aroused from his reverie by the arrival of Judge Stanleigh, slartled as he rode up bY the sound of a revolver shot. "What are you doing, man?" -; "I am Marmaduke Smythe," replied the fright- ened lawyer. "I thought I heard the stealthy approach of savage Indians." "Indians 1" echoed the Judge. "Why, my dear Mr. Smythe. it .is years since we saw one in this country. We are aristocrats here, sir, and have no use for Indians." "Deane me! then I must have been misinformed. I had always looked upon America as half-civilised and overrun with wild men," said Marmaduke. "Sir, you entirely misjudge us. Your mind is full of the wonderful story of that ancestor of ours who found the diamond. But, sir "—slapping his riding-boot with his whip—" even the Indians in those days were no match for a Stanleigh " ; and, breast swelling with pride. Judge Stanleigh mounted and rode home, Marmaduke following with arms firmly clasped around the neck of the horse he rode.

" Now, sir," said the Judge, after he had feasted the visitor in royal style, "Mrs. Arthur Stanleigh will shortly become a mother. In fact, I believe Dr. Henry Lee is even now at Stanleigh Hall. We, however, are not on friendly terms." "Quite so." replied Marmaduke, inaptly. "If a girl be born at Stanleigh Hall to-night, your child will be heir to the Earldom and the diamond. Despite this family feud, Colonel Arthur Stanleigh must summon us to verify the child being boy or "Yes, and I'll take jolly good care to be there," was the ungracious response.

• • • •

"A fine boy is born to the gipsy woman, an omen of the same good luck to Stanleigh Hall," was the greeting of Dr. Lee as he returned to the Colonel.

Restlessly Colonel Stanleigh paced his study. The fulfilment of the greatest desire of his life depended upon the few words his old friend would next utter. They came at last, pregnant with dire signi- ficance: " Stanleigh, it is a girl." "I am sorry for you, old man," said his doctor friend. " Maybe it is for the best."

"The best, that Lamar Stanleigh's son should rule as head of a noble family? The Stanleighs must have reached a pretty pass." Colonel Stanleigh's disappointment was terrible to witness, and the doctor, who had .left him to fight out the chagrin alone, returned to say that the young mother— paying the great sacrifice—asked for The Diamond from the Sky, the Stanleigh "charm against harm." "She shall not have it. The diamond is not for a air' to wear. No, by heaven, and a boy shall wear it," almost shouted the Colonel, as a desperate idea Came into his head.

• The dying mother could not be denied a last request, and, choking down his grief, the husband handed the feeble woman the charm. Fastening the goldeß chain from which the great diamond was pendent round the baby's neck, she sighed and passed away. "Lee. listen to me. I am determined to defeat Judge Stanleigh. You say Hagar the gipsy has a son? Then I'll buy it, rear the girl in secret, and spoil my cousin's ambition in gaining the title and diamond for the gipsy'a son." This was the mad idea that entered the Colonel's mind, and, seeing that he could not be dissuaded from it, Dr. Lee became a party to the fraud. Taking five thousand dollars from the safe, Colonel Stanleigh and Dr. Lee went to the gipsy encampment, and had little diffi- culty in inducing the money-greedy Matt Harding to sell his offspring. That part of the scheme safely accomplished, it was an easy matter to swear the coloured servants, heart and soul with the Colonel in his feud, to secrecy. _ _

" Go, tell Judge Stanleigh that a son is born at Stanleigh Hall," commanded the Colonel; and as Ned, the old butler, carried the intelligence to the Judge and Marmaduke Smythe, the negro nurse changed the diamond from little Esther to the neck of the usurper. When the exasperated Judge and the lawyer, be- wildered by the torrent of events and totally un- able to comprehend the violence of the feud, arrived at Stanleigh Hall, they were greeted by Colonel Stanleigh. "My wife is dead, gentlemen, but this child is my son, heir to the Earldom of Stanleigh and The Diamond from the Sky." Inwardly raging, the Judge signed the certificate of, identity. • When Matt Harding robbed his wife of her child and accepted the blood money from Col. Stanleigh he gagged and tied the woman to the tent pole. Despair and mother-love lent her strength, and within an hour she was free. Shrewdly guessing what had happened, Hagar stole from the tent. Quietly as she moved and quickly, her husband seized her and threw her to the ground. Five thousand dollars were worth more to him than the baby. "Lie there, you fool!" he hissed. "If you move I'll kill you." But Hagar bided her time, and as her brutal husband's back was turned she rose and felled him to the ground. Staggering and gasping she made for Stanleigh Hall. Reaching the door of the library a baby, her baby, was the centre of an aristocratic rite or cere- mony strange to her eyes. A great jewel glowed on his little breast. As Hagar pushed open the door to step into the room Colonel Stanleigh caught sight of her, and with a cry fell unconscious across the library table.
