IN THE COURTS.

and the whole of the people were very much upset.

-These facts came out at the Borough Police Court on Thursday, when Beetle was charged with falsely representing himself to be a special constable.

"I was a special constable on that occasion," he said. "Being an ex-soldier I thought it my duty to inform them that the light should not be burning so that it could be seen from out- side. I cannoth, however, remember saying I was a special constable."

In ordering him to pay ss. costs, the Chair- man said he had been guilty of too much zeal. "The very name of a policeman frightens some," be added. "I am oven frightened of them myself." (Laughter-)

WAITING FOR HIS PAPERS.

3larried Barman- Arrested Under Compulsion Act.

At the Borough Police Court, yesterday, Stanley James, 57, Longford-street, was sum- moned for being an absentee under the Mili- tary Service Act. When taken into custody by Special Constable Dendy defendant replied that he was waiting for his papers. The Chief Constable explained that the de- fendant was a married man and was formerly employed at Messrs. Rylands Bros., but he left with the consent of the firm to take up a posi- tion as barman. It was customary to send a personal notice, but it was not obligatory on the military authorities. The proclamation posted on the walls was all the notice that men were entitled to. Defendant's group was sum- moned on April 13th. _

Defendant. said that on his attestation card it was distinctly stated that a notice would be sent and he was waiting for the papers from the military authorities. The magistrates handed him over to the mili- tary authorities without the imposition of the usual fine of 40s.

BLACK MAN WARNS WOMEN ABOUT THEIR DAUGHTERS.

At the Borough Court on Saturday, Peter Johnson, a coloured man from Old Calabar, now residing at 14, Stanley-street, was fined :is. for assaulting a fellow country man named Alfred Edward Jones.

The Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls) said the trouble had arisen out of jealousy over some women living in the neighbourhood of Stanley-street. Complainant came to War- rington about a month ago, and took up his residence at 12, Stanley-street, but as he was anxious to get away from the other coloured men he changed his lodgings. He was followed to his new home by the defendant, Johnson, and some other men, who locked him in a room and assaulted him, Johnson knocking one of Jones' teeth out. ' Jones asserted that he bad given Johnson no provocation for the assault. Corroborating th 3 Chief Constable's statement, he said the trouble had started because he had warned married women in the neighbourhood not to allow their daughters to become acquainted with the de- fendant.

In reply to the magistrates, Johnson said he was a labourer at Dallam Forge.

STOLE BUILDERS' WATER TANK.

Though denying the theft when challenged by Detective-Sergeant Sumner, Michael Swift, of 7. Cloth Hall-yard, at the Borough Court on Mon- day, admitted that he had stolen an iron waLer tank, value gl., from premises at the rear of Buttermarket-street. The tank was the property of Messrs. W. and A. Ashton, builders, who were carrying out some work on the premises.

The Chief Constable added that the prisoner's wife took the tank td a marine store dealer and got 6s. 4d. for it after it had been broken up by her and her husband. Fined 405.; in default, a month's imprison- ment.

MOTORISTS WHO DRIVE TO THE DANGER

OF THE PUBLIC.

Half a dozen motorists hailing from Liver- pool, Manchester and Leigh, were fined various amounts at the Borough Police Court on Tues- day for driving dangerously along Liverpool- road or Knutsford-road.

In three cases the Bench decided not to hare the licences endorsed with convictions, but ordered them to put money into the poor box and pay the costs of the prosecutions.

SHIRKERS NOT ALL OF ONE COUNTRY.

When an Irishman, named James Mullahy, of no fixed abode, was charged at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday with being an absentee under the Military Service Act, the Chief Con- stable (Mr. Martin Nicholls) informed the Court that the prisoner absconded from Dallam Forge in January and turned up on Monday to ask for work there again. The Chief Constable was remarking that the prisoner departed, like many more of his country men, owing to con- scription, when The Chairman (Councillor Henry Roberts) intervened with the observation, "Don't say they are just of one country. There are some of other countries who are using every possible dodge to escape it." Fined 40s. and handed over to the military authorities.

THE TRIBUNALS.

ANOTHER BUSY AND VARIED WEEK.

By having frequent sittings the Warrington Tribunal is gradually getting through the heavy list of appeals which at one time threatened to inundate them. At Tuesday's\sitting.

A contractor and earner, aged 35, married, and four children, was granted four months' ex- emption. He stated he was engaged two days weekly in carting coal from the Winwick siding to the houses of attendants employed at Lord Derby War Hospital., On other days he was employed carrying goods to and from the markets held in neighbouring towns.

Not a Woman's Job.

Messrs. John Hammond and Co. appealed for the manager of their branch tailoring and clothiers' shop in Horsemarket-street. The appellant was the only adult employe in the shop, which had been established fifty years. It was obviously a man's work, as a woman could not measure 'Men for clothes. The manager was a married man with two children.—Conditional exemption was granted.

When One's Living Is At Stake.

Three. months' exemption (to be regarded as final) was granted to a dental mechanic, married, with three children, whose application was Made by his employer, Mr. Henry Griffin, also a pieture palace proprietor. Since the war Mr. Griffin stated that he had had to give up his dental practice to attend to the picture palace, as three of his men had gone into th? Army. "I feel ashamed to do this appeal busi- ness. I think every man ought to fight, but when one's living is at stake it is a serious problem," concluded Mr. Griffin. Leather Broker Exempted.

Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, appealed on behalf of Wm. Watson Eyre, leather factor, Academy- street,. for the exemption of his son, who was engaged in the business. One of the travellfts enlisted early in the war, and the son was doing his work, the traveller, who is an officer, re- ceiving his usual commission whilst he is away. —Conditional exemption was granted. Theatre Manager's Claim.

On behalf of Sir Joseph Beecham, proprietor of the Royal Court Theatre, application for exemption was made by Mr. A. Browne for the local manager. He stated that since the war 22 of the assistants had gone from the theatre, tw.) of, whom had been killed. The manager was practically the only man left. The new amuse- meat tax had caused increased "work, as some- thing like £2,000 per annum would have to be collected in small amounts. Sir Joseph Beecham could not possibly get anyone else to fill • the position, as it took years of training to get into the management of a theatre. All contracts were completed by the manager, and there was , a staff of 47 under him.—Conditional exemption was granted. A Solicitor Appeals For His Clerk.

An appeal was made by Mr. Browne for his own cashier and temporary managing clerk, aged 32 and married. Eormerly he had a staff ot eleven, but at present he had only six. He thought his office had done its shaFe with four members of the staff with the Colours, including his own son. He had one other clerk who had not attested.—The Chairman of the Tribunal (Dr. Joseph), in grantig conditional exemp- tion, said they were of the opinion that he only ought to have one manaiing clerk. A Newsagent's Claim.

A newsagent and Stationer, repiesented by Mr. Browne, stated his business would be ruined if he had to go, as his wife could not manage, her time being fully occupied in attending an in- valid child.—Exemption until the end of August was granted. Revenue Collectors As Munition Workers.

Mr. H. J. Westbrook, solicitor, appeared to support a claim for one of the principal clerks in " die office of Robert Davies and Co., solicitors. The Government, he stated, were alwiays pressing them to deliver moneys from the stamp, death and estate duties. He 'Con- sidered they were munition workers in. provid- ing money to pay for the war.—Appellant also had a personal business claim inasmuch since the death of his father, in his spare time, he hal had to manage a pot and china business for the other members of the family, who were bene- ficiaries. Three other brothers were in the business, one had joined the Colours, one :was over military age, and the third was appealing along ,with himself that day.—The Tribunal granted conditional exemption to the• brother who had the joint appeal, and exemption until. the end of September was granted to the brother engaged in the business,. Picture Palace Manager.

The claim of the manager of the Queen's Picture Palace, Orford-lane, was advocated li Mr. T. S. Steel, who stated that in audition to hi 4 duties as manager, the appellant was the electrical engineer, having sole charge of their own electric plant.—The decision on the busi- ness claim was adjourned, but appellant was granted until the end of September on a per- sonal appeal. Basket and Hamper Workers' Claims.

Mr. T. S. Steel appealed on behalf of Mr. Daniel Johnson, basket and hamper manufac- hirer, for six of his employes. The number was reduced to four in view of the fact that one of the appellants had joined the Naval Reserve, and another was exempted because he would at- tain the age of. 41 in a few weeks.—Of the four, two were conditionally exempted, and two were given until the end of September. when the fruit season would be over.' Prior to the outbreak if war there were 39 employes, 18 of whom joined the Forces, twelve of the remainder were over military.age. The work was chiefly for farmers and market gardeners, and fully 90 per cent. of the output was for agricultural purposes. An official of the Board of Agriculture had been to the works and urged the necessity of increasing the output. pirls could not do the work as it was too heavy for them.

===== 0002601_19160603_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
"COMBING OUT."

Reviewing Cases Of Unstarred Single Young Men.

"SPARE WAR BADGES."

The Tribunal. at their second sitting on Thursday afternoon, were engaged in "combing" out a number of the single men who were auto- matically exempted under the old reserved list of occupations. Since May Ist, in a number of reserved trades, no ,worker can claim reservatiqn under. the age- of 25. The sitting Was only a short one, and out, of eight appeals, six were refnsed. In,. the other cases ono .was granted temporary exemption, and one conditional exemption. A- single young Man, aged 20, against whom the military had•appealed as he was no lonlor starred; claimed that he' had his mother, two brothers and a sister to support. •, His fathom* lived away from home, and only sent money at irregular intervals.—The application was re- fused, ',the Chairman pointing but that the authorities would make the father support for those who were dependent: " ' '

An employe of -the , Ship Canal Co., engaged as a lock keeper.at-Latchford,'Claimecl eemption on domestic grounds. It was pointed sout to him that, as he was only 21 .years of ,age and single he could not be included in the list of certified occupations: ‘. When the National Registration was taken he was employed as .a' moulder at a local works. His widowed mother and two chil- dren were partially dependent upon him.—The appeal was refused. A clerk. aged 20, in the employ of the .Cheshire Lines Railway goods department, -Was also re- fused exemption. He. informed the Tribunal that the manager of, the department had told him to refer the , recruiting authorities to him whim he received a call.---LNà. aPPeal'had been

made by the company for the man to be badged.

" That Settles It!"

On behalf of the executors of a local carting contractor an appeal was made by the manager for a shoeilig smith, single, aged 20, who nad been in their employ for three years. The manager admitted that, he had some spare war badges at the office.

The Clerk: It is about time you sent them back again. Captain Mathams: I suppose they enhance in value every day.

The Appellant: They can have them back if they want them. He was under the impression that the youth was included in the 'starred list. The Chairman: No, he is only 20. He has to be over 25.

Appellant: That settles it! A railway carter, aged 22, formerly a painter, who applied last December to be placed in the first group of married men, was asked by the Clerk if he desired anything further, as the first groups of the married were now called up. Applicant: I should like to know what allow- ance my mother will receive. The Chairman: We are of the opinion that your case will come under the new Government scheme. The rent of the house will be paid. Conditional and Temporary Exemptions For

Milk Dealers.

A single milk dealer in business on his own account, aged 27, who has a motor in addition to a pony and float to deliver milk, was granted conditional exemption. Since his father's death five years ago, he had carried on the. business, having a dairy and a milk round. His only brother was in the Army. Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, who applied for exemption for another single milk dealer, age 21, stated that appellant was formerly a clerk, but acting on the advice of a doctor, he went in for an outdoor occupation, and purchased a milk business. Since then his health had im- proved.-

Three months' exemption was granted, to be regarded as final.

Mr. Browne intimated that efforts would be made to dispose of the business.

The appeal of an employe of the Ship Canal who did not appear in person to support his claim, but sent a letter from the Ship Canal Company dated January, claiming ext.lmptian under a certified occupation, was refused. Captain Mathams pointed out that since that letter the lists had been revised.

BOY SCOUTS' SECRETARY.

Manager Of Athletic Stores To

Use His Strength For Army.

At Friday's Tribunal: Himself serving in the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, the proprietor of a lubricant business sent a letter appealing for the exemption of the man he had left in charge. There was now a staff.Of four people, and if his deputy left the. business would have to. be closed down. The lubricants were mostly used by firms engaged on muni- tions. The case was adjourned for a week in order to obtain paiticplars as to turnover, &c. Making a further appeal for the manager, of the Athletic Stores, Market-place, Mr. W. J. Shaw said it was impossible, to replace him by a lady, owing to the character of the work. Be (Mr. Shaw) knew every than 'was wanted for the Army, but, unfortunately, theirs was one of those cases which was a one-man show. The manager, who was single, was also secretary of the Warrington Boy Scouts.. .The Chairman,: You ought to find someone for a stop-gap to carry on the shop. Mr. Shaw: Grant us further time, then, please. The Chairman : 'You have had four months already. We will give you another month, but that is ,final.

Through. Mr. A. Browne solicitor, a driver- traveller for Messrs. Naylor and CO., confec- tioners', was conditionally exempted, and an- other was granted a Month's: respite. Mr. Browne mentioned that, the firm had four .horses grazing idly through lack of drivers. A Stake in the Country.

Urging that he should be exempted in order ta carry on a business in which his two soldier brothers were interested, a paper merchant and stationer said that if the business had to be closed down it was not much encouragement either for him or his brothers to do their share in the Army.—Decision adjourned for a week. An under-manager in a public-house, married, with one. child, appealed on domestic grounds. Mr. T. S. Steel said that for some time the wife of the appellant had been subject to nervous breakdown. The claim as solely on account 4 his wife's health.—Decision was adjourned for further medical evidence. • • , •

A violinist in the Palace and Hippodrome band, appealed to be dealt with in the same way as other members of the profession—recom- mended to a branch of the service where his fingers would not be damaged. The Chairman (Mr. F. A. Frost) said the applicant had had the advantage of the delay which had taken place in regard to the case of another appellant. They had decided to treqt him in the same way and refuse the application. If he desired to preserve his fingers he ought to apply for the Navy Signalling Service, whien would not hurt his fingers. No Financial Hardship.

The domestic appeal of a soap stamper at Messrs. Crosfierds, a single man, aged 33, was refused. He claimed to be the sole support of his sister aged 46. He had been before the Tribunal as a conscientious objector and his claim was disallpwed. In dismissing the appeal, the Chairman said applicant could get an allowance from the Government for the house anti an allotment for his sister.

A mastei window cleaner claimed exemption on business grounds. Tie stated he bought the business twelve months ago, and there was still an amount owing. Ile wag a moulder pre- viously.

The Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway): I suppaq, you ,knew the risks you were incurring when you bought the business: Applicant: When you speculate there is alway, a risk. I lose the business if I go, besides being faced with a debt if I come back. I have tried to 'sell it and failed.

The Chairman: The anplication is refuses], but we wish to point out that the balance owing will be provided for by the Government.

~ Applicant: I can appeal -at Liverpool I sup- pose F—Yes. Messrs. .1. and W. STOCKTON HEATH DISTR ICT.

£7,000 Worth Of Orchids In Danger Of Being Lost.

I.M. MINISTER MAKES ENTANGLE-

MENTS.

Several appeals from the Warrington district were dealt with by the Runcorn Rural Tribunal at the Workhouse, Dutton. on Thursday. Mr. C. E. Linakei, sen., presided. Stockton Heath Orchids.

Mr. Jeans, solicitor, stated that if a Stockton Heath. professional oichid grower were sent to the Army £7,000 worth of orchids would be lost. The man was suffering from an 'injury to the foot and could not walk very far. He had been certified as fit for garrison duty at home or abroad. _ _ _

The Chairman: What you have fo decide is the fate of the nation against £7,000 worth of orchids. ,

A Member: The employer does not grow any vegetables. He has 'a chauffeur under military age, medically unfit. I think the man ought to go.

The appeal was not allowed. Independent Methodist Minister's _Conditional

Exemption.

A Stockton Heath man, employed at War- rington as a wire entanglement maker, asked for absolute exemption on the ground that he is an Independent Methodist minister. - His case had been before the Warrington Tribunal, which had granted 'him the "conditional exemp- tion, but he made .the present application be- cause if he left his work he would be liable for service in the Army. The Chairman: This man is not a minister; he is a wire entanglement maker. The Tribunal considered it sufficient that conditional exemption had been allowed at Warrington and refused to allow the appeal. 'Sir Harmood Banner's Only Chauffeur.

Mr. H. N. Linaker, solicitor, appeared in support of an appeal for Sir J. S. Harmood Banner's "chauffeur. He said. that the other chauffeur and the car cleaner had enlisted, and all the footmen, gardeners, keepers, far- mers, mechanics and horsemen had attested. The men he still retained had either been rejected or•were not of military age. A letter was read from Sir Harmood Banner, mentioning the many directorships held by him, and stating that it was quite impossible for him to attend to his duties without a com- petent chauffeur who knew the locality. Exemption until the Ist of August A Deserved Exemption.

"I only get about three hours sleek a day," said a Halton man. From 6 p.m. until 6 a.m. he was on duty as a civil guard, guarding Norton Water Tower, , and in the daytime he followed the occupation of an hotel assistant. The Military Representative: He ought to be in the Army if he can do with as little sleep as that. (Laughter) The Chairman : You will have exemption for as long as you are guarding Norton Water Tower and only sleeping three hours a day. (Laughter.)

Killing and Burying.

"If the young man is as well qualified to kill Germans as the old man is to bury parish- ioners he will do all right in the Army," said the Chairman in reference to the appeal of a Great Budworth stonemason and sexton.—Ap- pellant said that his father had just given up the office of sexton, in consequence of advanc- ing years.—Exemption until. the Ist July. In. the Clerical Department.

Mr. Jeans, solicitor, Warrington, , appealed for exemption for the cashier and salesman of the Grappenhall .Tanning C 0.,. Ltd.—Mr: Jeans said the man appealed- for had not been badged because, presumably, he was in the clerical department. —Exemption Until Ist September.

Butcher's Clerk Refused.

Grappenhall Cowman.

A GrUppenhall cowman was exempted until Ist September. Greyhounds Not Of National Importance.

The Tribunal decided that the training of greyhounds is not of national importance and exempted a Preston Brook greyhound trairier and farmer only till Ist August. The farm was one of eleven acres and appellant was the only man left on it.—Appellant said that if he had to join the Army g 8 greyhounds would have to be destroyed. Plumbers Exempted. •

A Stockton Heath master plumber and pain- ter was given conditional exemption, and an employe of his, a sanitary plumber, was ex- empted until the Ist July.

POULTRY FOR PROFIT. SEASONABLE HINTS. At this season the poultry plant will be fullY taxed. Every house will be utilised. and the land will, be taxed to its utmost capacity. A good supply of food troughs is a necessity. Chickens will not thrive if they cannot feed in com- fort. Extra water troughs and grit boxes will be necessary. The lime-washing might be started now. Select a warm day and do the work in the morning. The wall will then be dry when the birds go to roost. Leave all ventilators and doors wide. open d.uring the day. The early cockerels, when intended for exhibition, should, be shaded at the age of three months. Do not rely on natural shade alonei. White birds should be shaded between 7.30 a.m. and 4 p.m. Before and after these times the sun is not powerful enough to affect the colour.

been left behind in far off lands, playing in I the fields' where I suppose we will take the largest part of our fun. Many will be in black this year, and those who have had such great losses will recount the pleasures of the past wherein they had most happy times together." Niece Elsie sends a new member, and hopes I shall be pleased. I am. Niece Maggie Cawley, 3, Goulden-street (Arpley-street School).—" The processioes through the town will not be quite as joyful and gay as in times before the European Wa:, be- cause nearly all the bands will be needed for regimental purposes. The small children will be taken around the town and to the field in lurries, while the older ones will either walk or go by train. Children are benefited by Walk- ing Day, because visiting different places each year, they become broad-minded and are able to see much more of the world for themselves." Niece Jenny Dunlop, 52, Chester-road (Arpley-street School).—" We are all glad there is going to be a Walking Day this year. It is a beautiful sight to see the little children walk- ing. We 'could economise in dress, perhaps, but we like to see the little children pretty. We can hardly do away with the amusements and the stalls in the field, as these are mostly what the children look forward to. I cannot really think of how we are going to economise if the children are to have a happy, enjoyable day." Niece Dorothy Wright, 68, Winwick-street (Wycliffe School).—" I am glad we are having Walking Day this year, and I suppose prepara- tions will be made at Sunday schools for it. We had field dais before this war started, and grand ones, too. We are having Walking Day this year because all the soldiers wanted it, and when they are lying in the hospital they will know that their own children will be able to enjoy themselves." Nephew Albert Bloomfield (Heathside School). —" When I get on the field I will have a game of football and I will spend 'very little money. Some boys will be extravagant and spend all their money as soon as they get on the field, but I hope the children of the Corner will not be extravagant. When I have had my tea 1 will play other games, and then I will come home with the other boys." Niece Alice Boyle, 333, Wellfield-street.—"We must not only observe Walking Day this time as a joyous time, but we must think of those men that played the bands or held the banners and are now soldiers, and fighting for the right and liberty of the English people. We must see if we cannot send them some little token to show that the Warrington people think of them still." WON THREE PRIZES. "52, Chester-road, Warrington, May 28th. "Dear Uncle Jack,—Thank you so much for the beautiful book I received on Saturday morning. I have now three books, two of which I won in Uncle Ned's time. I am sure it will be a most interesting book, but I have not started to read it yet.—From your loving niece, JENNY DUNLOP?' LANCE-CORPORAL A. RILEY. Killed.

PDIVATE J. lIEESOM„ D.C.M. The announcement which we made last week to the effect that Private Joseph Heesom, of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, son of .Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Heesom, of 31, Orford-avenue, had won the D.C.M., was officially confirmed on Tuesday, when his name appeared amongst a list of awards for gallantry in the field, published in the "London Gazette." The,deed for which he won the distinction was as follows : For conspicuous gallantry as leading bomber of a raiding party. He was the first into the enemy's trench, and did fine work with his bombs. He had previously been noted for plucky conduct. Private Heesom, who is only 18 years of age, was formerly employed by the Lorigford Wire Co., and later by Messrs. Rylands Bros. Ltd. He is an old boy of the Beamont School, and attended the Brick-street Mission Sunday School. His brother, Henry, has served the necessary time in the local Territorials. Our Motor Ambulance Fund. A further list of subscriptions towards the fund for providing Warrington with a motor ambulance will be found in our advertisement columns., koney is urgently needed, and we hope the townspeople will rally, to the call. It ía for a good object, for the motor ambulance will be used for the conveyance of wounded warriors to keal hoSpitals, and at the end of the war will be retained for -use in the borough. . . . .•

OUR TERRITORIALS. Interest In Walking Day And Motor Ambulance. HAPPY DAY FOR KIDDIES. A Warrington Territorial writing from the Front assures the Editor that the arrival of The Examiner" is always welcome. "We are,", he continues, "stationed not far behind the line. "'The Runs have been doing a fair amount of strafing this morning with • their whiz-bangs. The only visitors we have are., rats, shells and bullets. Not very nice companions, are. they? So long as .Fritz leaves us alone we don't mind. You would laugh if you saw some of the notices lon• some of the men's billets, which conSist of cellars. I was walking past one Place, and in big white chalk letters printed on the door was 'No shells admitted in here except on business.'. "I see by 'The Examiner' I got last night that the-towrspeople are going to have a' Walk- ing .Day.' I can assure you we, .the lads from 'Warrington, are all very, very 'pleased, and we wish the heads who are working this* happy day for the kiddies every success. • "I also see that you are trying 'to scrape enough money to buy 'another ambulanee. if people.could only see the work these cars do out here they would not think twice. I think this is. all Ibis time, except that ..the boys of the `}gburth are just as cheerful BB ever."

LANCE-CRPL. J. ANTROBUS.' Reported Missing After Heavy Bombardment. Mrs. Antrebus, Yeald Brow, Canal Bank, has this week received a letter from one of her son's comrades, notifying her that her son, Lance- Corporal Joseph Antrobus, of the Cheshire Regiment, is reported missing. The letter reads as follows :—" I am writing these few lines on behalf of your son who, I am sorry to say, is reported missing after opera- tions last Sunday, the 21st inst. We are all living in hopes that we shall very shortly hear of him being safe and sound. There are a lot of fellows we have not been able to get in yet, but we are doing so as fast as we possibly can under the present conditions. I am sorry that I have not been able to inform you definitely before, but I have been making inquiries about him since last Sunday night. Yesterday I was in- formed that he and Fred Moss, of Lymm, Were reported missing, so to confirm this I went out this afternoon to find out where the remainder of the battalion was, and also to see the other fellows from Lymm, and to find out if this re- port was correct. I found T. and W. Read and Percy Appleton (all Lymm lads), and they told me that it was correct, so I thought it my duty as a comrade to inform you at once. We are all deeply grieved at having to report this, and I am sure we shall miss them greatly, for

they had three drivers altogether, but they were considerably understaffed. One was over mili- tary age, one was medically unfit, and the other was the one appealed for. They had recently purchased two new horses, which were young , and high spirited, and wanted a lot of manag- ing. Could not the fire engine be driven by one of the other men ?—The horses are young and high spirited. One of the drivers is lame, and there- fore would not be active enough, and the other is fifty-six years of age. How is it that the Council now use their ow-n horses for the fire engine?—The man who guaranteed horses for the fire engine wished to withdraw his offer owing to being short of labour. How was the Council work performed before you got horses of your own F—By hired carters, but we got a great many complaints about refuse not being taken away early enough in a morning. Exemption till June 26th was granted. The Surveyor was granted conditional exemp- 'lion on the grounds that he was indispensable and that it was in the public interest that he should remain at his work. It was stated that owing to enlistments he was at the present time entirely without staff. Can Women Paint? A painter and decorator appealed for his son, 28, married, on the grounds that he was indis- pensable, and was the main support of two families. Before the war he had three work- men, but now had only the son to rely on. By the Chairman: How long has he been working for you F—Since he left school. How many men had you last year?—One be- sides the son. What steps have you taken to get labour?— Applied to the Labour Exchanges. Have painters ever tried to get women P—No , I don't think they would prove efficient, especially when they had ladders to climb. Do you do a certain amount of work yourself ? —Yes, when there is no climbing to do. Applicant, who added that painters had t) make enough money during the summer months to tide them over the winter, was granted con- ditional exemption till September Ist. Dairyman's Successful Appeal. A dairyman (36), married, appealed for exemption, stating that if he had to go it would mean the loss of his life savings in business, which amounted to £240, and £4O worth of debts which he had out. He had been in the busi- ness three years. He had tried to sell the busi- ness and had advertised it three times. He had never tried to get anyone to do the work.-- Conditional exemption until August 26th. Farmer Appeals For Son. A farmer appealed for his son (18), on the grounds that he was indispensable to the work- ing of the farm. His farm comprised 50 acres of land, 17 acres of which was arable, three horses, one colt, and 14 cows and heifers. He himself was away all the week, and only a home for week-ends. His son had been on th&. farm since he left school, and his duties at the present time consisted of managing, supervising and actually working on the farm. Ile had two sons serving with the Colours.—The Military Representative: I gather that this is not your main interest in life?—Applicant: No! It's an accident. Conditional exemption until October Ist was granted. GUNNER CHARLES HARTLEY. Wounded In Hand While With Maxim Guns. Gunner Charles Hartley, Cheshire Regiment, is at present in a base hospital suffering from a wound in the hand. Gunner Charles Hartley is very well known to Lymm people, having been employed as gardener to Sir Frederick Smith during the time that he occupied Lymm Hall. He enlisted in September, 1914, and was drafted out to France in September, 1915. He is highly respected amongst his comrades, several of whom are Lymm lads. At the time that he received his wound he was serving with the Maxim gun section.

Another Warrington Case: On January 6th, 1913, Mr. J. Lakin, of 16, Holland-street, Warrington, said:—" I work in a hot place, and coming out into the cold air was the cause of my kidney trouble. I was at- tacked by acute pains in the region of the kidneys, which seemed to drag me down. As my work is a strain on the back these pains made it diffi- cult for me. "The water was in an unnatural state, and I knew from this my kidneys were affected. "I tried Doan's Backache Kidney Pills for this complaint, and proved them entirely satisfactory. After taking the pills the water became clear again and my back was free from pain. Since then I have usually kept a box in the house and use them if I feel any signs of the old trouble. (Signed) "J. Lakin." On March 15th, 1915—over two years later— Mr. Lakin said :—" I have kept well since I used Doan's Pills. They cured me splendidly." ANGLING. WARRINGTON WORKING MEN'S ASSOCIATION. N.A. CENTRE. AnOther week has passed without any trout Pass- ing over the scales. Members turned out in num- bers to visit the Gowy. Dace, roach and perch were hooked in fair numbers, and had to be re- turned. One-Member took four dace in about half an hour while fishing for trout. Had the season been open ono in particular would have beers.regis: tered. We notice there are a fair• number—of perch' in the Mill Race. These were well on feed. and•io. good condition, but we never have any of note during the open season. Probably it is because' the worm is discarded for the maggot. The latter is a more eure bait than the former. The Opening Match. The opening match of the season is to be fished at Aintree in the Leeds and Liverpool Canal on June 25th. The reason for bringing this match for- ward is to give members a chance of taking part in a contest before joining up in His Majesty's Forces. This matter was decided at a general meeting held at headquarters on Monday. It was also decided i that all who take part in the contest must be fully paid up or entry will be refused. The Secretary pointed out to the members that this was neces- sary to ensure the financial position of the club. The treasurer also remarked that it would make matters easier for the officials if this was carried out. This ruling must not be confused with other affiliated clubs, as it only applies to the centre. Other clubs make their own rules for contests. Delegate Meeting. The Executive Council meets to-day (Saturday) at Farnworth, near Bolton. We predict a good attendance of delegates, as there are some very important items on the agenda. There are waters to consider, as well as the financial position, the charge for members of centres to be raised for two years, and other matters that will be reported on later. NATIONAL FEDERATION OF ANGLERS. Gift of Motor Ambulances. The annual conference was held at Leeds, repre- sentatives being in attendance from most associa- tions. Lieut.-Colonel Maxwell Telling, represent- ing the War Office, was present to thank the work- ing men anglers for their handsome gift. He regretted that one of the vehicles could not be allotted to the district of which he had charge. The delegates then inspected one of the cars, which the War Office had forwarded for inspection. Obtaining Stock Fish. On resumption of business the N.A.A. delegate (Mr. Lees, of Oldham) put the following proposi- tion before the meeting: "That owing to the diffi- culty associations have in obtaining stock fish, the General Purposes Committee shall obtain informa- tion from Conservancy Boards and owners of trout waters where they intend to net coarse fish, and shall endeavour to make arrangements for securing; and storing the fish so netted ,in suitable watera,, such waters to be loaned to,, or rentear.l33l.. be Federation. The fish• obtained —to be offered' for sale to the members of the N.F.A. only, at a remunerative price, all profits to be added to the Federation funds." This is the second time the delegates of the N.A.A. have had this proposition before the N.F.A., and although the scheme was not carried at this meeting, it was been to be worth considering, and we shall not be surprised if it bears fruit in the near future. It must be admitted by associations who have had great difficulty this season in obtaining stock fish, that if the proposition is not carried in its entirety, something must come of such a scheme to enable associations to purchase stock fish, as the difficulty has put many to inconvenience and expense. ORAL FICTION. Clever Rogue Professes To Be Mason And Army Officer. WANTED TRIAL IN CAMERA. It was obvious before John Drummond, of Scottish nationality and no settled abode, had been in the dock many minutes at the Warring- ton Policek Coat+ err.Thnrsday‘i,that, he, was most ntellifrentriiird 'one, 7not' •Sarprieed to:hear .the(liaiberan Rymer Yelling) rainaik‘at rthee'conelusion,:of -the. ease that it ws & Pity the•wisaiwn.-had: not used hie, wits; to -better , purpose. •.- - • • , The charges against the prisoner were of fail- ing to produce a National Registration Card, and of obtaining ss. by false pretences from Mr. Walter Ainsworth, Freemasons' almoner. The evidence was that the prisoner, a well- set and erect man of about 50 years of age, called at the works of Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd., and saw Mr. Wadsworth about employment, as he had walked from Manchester. He told Mr. Wadsworth that he was a Freemason, and Mr. Wadsworth, therefore, referred him to Mr. Ainsworth, saying that he would probably render him assistance. Prisoner interviewed Mr. Ainsworth at his shop in Bridge-street, tell- ing him that he belonged to the " Neptune " Lodge, No. 22, Chester, and, on that, Mr. Ains- worth gave ss. to the prisoner. It had since been ascertained that there was no " Neptune " Lodge at Chester. Later, prisoner went 1-,) several public-houses, particularly around Bank Quay, and there told a long tale to several People. One man took such a liking to prisoner that he asked him to come home with him, and there gave him the dinner which had been pre- pared for himself. After dinner, the prisona- asked his host to go out and get him some to- bacco, and while he was away the prisoner used disgraceful language to the ladies in the house, with the result that his observations were resented, and he was sent away. While in the public-houses he made inquiries as to the par- ticular kind of work which was going on at certain places, with the consequence that sus- picions were aroused, and through the instru- mentality of Mr. Murray, manager of Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co.. he was arrested by Detective-Sergeant Reddington. who asked him to produce his Registration Card, which was not forthcoming. Rusty On Masonic Rites. In the witness box Mr. Ainsworth remarked that the prisoner represented himself to be a Freemason, remarking that he had been a Past Master. Mr. Ainsworth -tested him, and told him that he knew very little about it, adding that: he w4as rusty on. Masonic-rites. .. The • Prisoner:, I went :through' the whole •Ptg4PlArlaali4, with the eXteßtitiaiatona question. I am a Mason. I have been so for many years, although I admit that I made a false statement to him when I said I belonged to the "Neptune"' Lodge at Chester. The Chief Constable (to Mr. Ainsworth): Is it usual for a Freemason to go into public- houses shouting out the fact that he is a Fr-?e- mason ?—No. A Mason would not act in that way at all. The Chief Constable: If he is a Past Master is it likely he would fail in the questions you put to him ?—No. Hereabouts. the prisoner protested that the case was being heard in open Court, contending that as he was a Freemason it should be heard in private. "If any of you gentlemen on the Bench are Masons you can test me in private and you will find I am all right." The Magistrates' Clerk (Mr. G. F. White): We don't try Cases in private here. The Chief Constable added that a Mason made a similar request to a judge at the Crimitril Court some time ago, but his appeal was turned down. Mr. Murray said that while in conversation with him, the prisoner said that besides being a Mason he had been a Major in the Lancashire Fusiliers, and had a son fighting at the Front. He had another son in Canada, he was a descendant of the Drummonds of Drummond Castle, and had land in Canada. •Aneounting .for the abseneeot his Registration .C.?.rd; I;)r,isonttr Said' I*9 in. PADGATE. Boy Scouts.—The Second Padgate Troupe of - Boy Scouts, .urt4er,7.the leadirship of Mr. W. E. Plinston, SeoUtmaster, paid a visit to •Frod- sham on Saturday afternoon. About 30 of the ' members assembled at• the Arpley Cannons, where they were met by a number of the mem- bers of Crosfield's troupe and together marched - to Bank Quay Station and then to Frodsham. - The Padgate troupe visited the National Chil- dren's. Home, where tea was provided, after which they again joined with Messrs. Crosfield's " troupe and the Frodsham troupe. A sham at- tack was planned by the three contingents, which proved a source of pleasure and enjoy- ment to all the boys. There was brilliant Slllll shine during the afternoon, and the boys thoroughly enjoyed the outing. Young Leaguers' Union.—On Saturday after- noon about thirty to forty members, including parents and friends, paid a visit to the 'National Children's Home and Orphanage at Frodsham. Tea was provided, after which the visitors were invited by the Superintendent to inspect the Homes, and especially to show them an orphan baby of five months old which the institution had taken charge of. The long walk to the Homes was rather tiresome to some of the younger children. FEARNHEAD. Risley Independent Methodists.—The Sunday school anniversary services in connettion with this place were held on Sunday, when sermons were preached afternoon and evening by Mr. C. Thornton, of Ashton-under-Lyne. .In the morn- ing Mr. J. Jeffs, of Fearnhead, delivered an address to the parents and scholars. The chil- dren, under the conductorship of Mr. J. Barton, sang special music at all the services, the choir also rendering the anthem, "Praise ye the Lord "at the evening service. The ser- vices were well-attended, and the eollectione amounted to over £lO. Mr. Percy Bate was the organist.
